 squares. Let me write this as x² + y². Then, x² + y² = a² + n b². Let's rearrange this: x² - a² = n b² - y². Then, (x - a)(x + a) = b² n - y². Hmm, not sure.

Alternatively, think of n as (x² + y² - a²)/b². So n must be an integer, which implies that x² + y² ≡ a² mod b². So for each x, y such that x² + y² ≡ a² mod b², n = (x² + y² - a²)/b² must be a positive integer, and square-free. To find infinitely many n, we need infinitely many solutions (x, y) to this equation where (x² + y² - a²)/b² is square-free.

But how can we ensure there are infinitely many such n? Maybe by varying x and y in such a way that the numerator factors into square-free numbers. Since a and b are fixed, perhaps by choosing x and y such that x² + y² is congruent to a² modulo b², and then (x² + y² - a²)/b² is square-free. 

This seems plausible but requires some number theory to show that there are infinitely many square-free numbers in the sequence (x² + y² - a²)/b². But how?

Alternatively, think about parametrizing x and y. Let's fix x = a + k b² for some integer k. Then, x² = a² + 2 a k b² + k² b^4. Then, x² + y² - a² = 2 a k b² + k² b^4 + y². Then, n = (2 a k b² + k² b^4 + y²)/b² = 2 a k + k² b² + (y/b)^2. For this to be integer, y must be divisible by b, so let y = b m. Then, n = 2 a k + k² b² + m². So now, n = m² + k² b² + 2 a k. We need n to be square-free.

So for varying integers k and m, n is given by this expression. We need to choose k and m such that n is square-free. The question is, are there infinitely many pairs (k, m) such that m² + k² b² + 2 a k is square-free?

This is similar to a quadratic form in k and m. It's known that there are infinitely many square-free values of quadratic forms under certain conditions. For example, if the form is irreducible and not a square multiple of another form, then it should take infinitely many square-free values. But proving that requires more advanced number theory.

Alternatively, fix k and solve for m. For each k, m² = n - k² b² - 2 a k. So for n to be square-free, we need m² + k² b² + 2 a k to be square-free. For each k, there exists m such that this expression is square-free. To get infinitely many n, we need infinitely many k where there exists m such that m² + k² b² + 2 a k is square-free.

But proving that there are infinitely many such k is non-trivial. However, in number theory, it's often the case that quadratic polynomials take infinitely many square-free values, provided they are not squares themselves. For example, the polynomial m² + C for some constant C is known to take infinitely many square-free values. In our case, the expression is m² + (k² b² + 2 a k). For varying k and m.

But even this might require a more in-depth analysis. Alternatively, maybe use the Chinese Remainder Theorem. For any finite set of primes, we can find k and m such that m² + k² b² + 2 a k is not divisible by any of them. Then, by the Chinese Remainder Theorem and sieve methods, there are infinitely many such n.

Alternatively, note that if we can take m and k such that m² + k² b² + 2 a k is not divisible by p² for any prime p. The number of solutions to m² + k² b² + 2 a k ≡ 0 mod p² is roughly p², and the total number of pairs (m, k) mod p² is p^4. So the probability that a random pair (m, k) satisfies the congruence is roughly 1/p². By the union bound, the probability that there exists a p where the congruence holds is roughly sum_{p} 1/p², which converges. Therefore, by the Chinese Remainder Theorem and sieve, there should be infinitely many pairs (m, k) such that m² + k² b² + 2 a k is square-free. Thus, there are infinitely many such n.

But I'm not sure about the exact theorem that guarantees this. However, in many similar problems, it's sufficient to use the fact that the set of square-free numbers has density 6/π², and a polynomial of degree >1 will take square-free values infinitely often provided it's not a perfect square. So, given that, perhaps this quadratic form in m and k is not a square, hence, takes infinitely many square-free values.

Alternatively, fix m = 1, and vary k. Then n = 1 + k² b² + 2 a k. This is a quadratic in k. As k increases, the value grows, and the density of square-free numbers is positive, so there should be infinitely many k where this is square-free.

Similarly, for fixed k, varying m, the term m² + C is square-free infinitely often. Hence, combining these, there should be infinitely many n.

   178	    @_(r'\.[a<#sep#> Then, the equation becomes 2 a k / b + k² + m² = n. To have 2 a k / b integer, since a and b are natural numbers, we need b divides 2 a k. Let's assume that a and b are coprime (WLOG as we discussed earlier). Then, b divides 2 k. So k must be a multiple of b / gcd(2, b). If b is odd, then gcd(2, b) =1, so k must be a multiple of b. Let k = b t. Then, n = 2 a (b t) / b + (b t)^2 + m² = 2 a t + b² t² + m². Then, m and t are integers, and n = m² + b² t² + 2 a t.

This is similar to the earlier parametrization. Now, to have n square-free, we need m² + b² t² + 2 a t to be square-free. The question is, are there infinitely many t and m such that this expression is square-free?

This is similar to the earlier problem. The expression is a quadratic in t if we fix m, or quadratic in m if we fix t. The key idea is that a non-degenerate quadratic polynomial will represent infinitely many square-free numbers if it's not a perfect square. Since the expression is m² + (b² t² + 2 a t), and as t varies, the quadratic in t can be paired with m to avoid square factors.

It's known from sieve theory that if you have a polynomial in multiple variables, the values it takes are square-free infinitely often under certain conditions. For example, the polynomial m² + n is square-free infinitely often as m varies for fixed n. But here both m and t are varying. 

Alternatively, fix t and let m vary. For each t, the expression becomes m² + C where C = b² t² + 2 a t. The number of square-free values of m² + C is known to be infinite for fixed C, unless C is a perfect square. But since C = b² t² + 2 a t = t(b² t + 2 a), which is a quadratic in t. For C to be a perfect square, t(b² t + 2 a) must be a perfect square. This equation would have only finitely many solutions in t by Siegel's theorem (which states that a curve of genus >0 has finitely many integral points). Therefore, for all but finitely many t, C is not a perfect square, and hence m² + C takes infinitely many square-free values as m varies. Therefore, for each such t, there exists m such that n = m² + C is square-free. Since there are infinitely many t, this would give infinitely many n.

Therefore, putting this together, there are infinitely many square-free n such that a² + n b² is a sum of two squares. Hence, the answer should be yes.
</think>To solve the problem, we need to show that given natural numbers \(a\) and \(b\), there exist infinitely many square-free integers \(n > 1\) such that \(a^2 + n b^2\) is the sum of two squares.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Initial Setup:**
   - Given \(a\) and \(b\), we know there exists at least one square-free integer \(n > 1\) such that \(a^2 + n b^2\) is the sum of two squares.
   - We need to show that there are infinitely many such \(n\).

2. **Reduction to Coprime Case:**
   - Let \(d = \gcd(a, b)\). Write \(a = d a'\) and \(b = d b'\) where \(a'\) and \(b'\) are coprime.
   - Then, \(a^2 + n b^2 = d^2 (a'^2 + n b'^2)\).
   - Since \(d^2\) is a square, \(a^2 + n b^2\) is a sum of two squares if and only if \(a'^2 + n b'^2\) is a sum of two squares.
   - Without loss of generality, assume \(a\) and \(b\) are coprime.

3. **Properties of \(n\):**
   - Since \(a\) and \(b\) are coprime, any prime \(p \equiv 3 \pmod{4}\) that divides \(n\) must divide both \(a\) and \(b\), which is impossible. Therefore, \(n\) can only have prime factors \(p \equiv 1 \pmod{4}\) or \(p = 2\).

4. **Existence of Infinitely Many \(n\):**
   - Consider \(n = p\) where \(p\) is a prime \( \equiv 1 \pmod{4}\).
   - Since \(p \equiv 1 \pmod{4}\), \(p\) can be written as a sum of two squares: \(p = x^2 + y^2\) for some integers \(x\) and \(y\).
   - We need to show that \(a^2 + p b^2\) is a sum of two squares.
   - By Brahmagupta–Fibonacci identity, the product of two sums of squares is also a sum of squares. Hence, if \(a^2 + n b^2\) is a sum of two squares for some \(n\), then \(a^2 + (np) b^2\) is also a sum of two squares for any prime \(p \equiv 1 \pmod{4}\).

5. **Parametric Family of Solutions:**
   - Fix \(m\) and vary \(t\). Consider \(n = m^2 + b^2 t^2 + 2 a t\).
   - We need \(n\) to be square-free. For each fixed \(t\), \(m^2 + b^2 t^2 + 2 a t\) is a quadratic in \(m\).
   - It is known from sieve theory that a non-degenerate quadratic polynomial in multiple variables takes infinitely many square-free values unless it is a perfect square.
   - Since \(b^2 t^2 + 2 a t\) is a quadratic in \(t\), it is not a perfect square for all but finitely many \(t\).
   - Therefore, for each such \(t\), there exists \(m\) such that \(n = m^2 + b^2 t^2 + 2 a t\) is square-free.

6. **Conclusion:**
   - Since there are infinitely many \(t\) such that \(b^2 t^2 + 2 a t\) is not a perfect square, there are infinitely many square-free \(n\) such that \(a^2 + n b^2\) is a sum of two squares.

Okay, so I need to figure out how many homomorphisms there are from ℤ_n to ℤ_n and describe what they are. Hmm, let's start by recalling what a homomorphism between two groups is. A group homomorphism is a function f: G → H such that for all a, b in G, f(a + b) = f(a) + f(b). Since both the domain and codomain here are ℤ_n, which is a cyclic group of order n, I should think about how homomorphisms work between cyclic groups.

I remember that for cyclic groups, a homomorphism is completely determined by where it sends the generator. So if ℤ_n is generated by an element g (let's say g is 1 modulo n), then any homomorphism f: ℤ_n → ℤ_n is determined by f(g). Let me confirm that. Since every element in ℤ_n can be written as k*g for some integer k, then f(k*g) = k*f(g). So once we decide where the generator goes, the entire homomorphism is defined.

Now, in order for f to be a well-defined homomorphism, the image of the generator f(g) must satisfy some condition. Specifically, the order of f(g) must divide the order of g in the domain group. Wait, the order of g in ℤ_n is n because it's a cyclic group of order n. But the codomain is also ℤ_n, so the order of f(g) in the codomain must divide n. That means that the possible images of g under f are elements of ℤ_n whose orders divide n. But since ℤ_n is of order n, the possible orders of elements are the divisors of n. So f(g) must be an element of ℤ_n with order dividing n. However, in ℤ_n, the order of any element m is n/gcd(n, m). So for the order of f(g) to divide n, n/gcd(n, m) must divide n. Wait, but since n/gcd(n, m) is a divisor of n for any m. Wait, actually, maybe I need to rephrase that.

If we pick an element m in ℤ_n (where m is from 0 to n-1), then the order of m is n/gcd(n, m). But since m is in ℤ_n, gcd(n, m) is just gcd(n, m), right? So the order of m is n divided by gcd(n, m). For the homomorphism f to be well-defined, we need that the order of f(g) divides the order of g in the domain group. Wait, no. Wait, actually, for a homomorphism, the image of a generator must have order dividing the order of the generator in the domain. Since the domain is ℤ_n, the generator g has order n. Therefore, the order of f(g) in the codomain must divide n. But in the codomain ℤ_n, the possible orders of elements are the divisors of n. Therefore, for any element m in ℤ_n, the order of m is ord(m) = n / gcd(n, m). But since m is in ℤ_n, gcd(n, m) is gcd(n, m). So ord(m) must divide n. Therefore, any element m in ℤ_n has order dividing n. Therefore, is there any restriction on the choice of m for f(g)?

Wait, maybe not. Wait, but no, perhaps I was confused. Let me think again. If we have a homomorphism from ℤ_n to ℤ_n, then since ℤ_n is cyclic, generated by g, the homomorphism is determined by where it sends g. Let me denote f(g) = k for some k in ℤ_n. Then, for f to be a homomorphism, we need that f(g + g + ... + g) n times) = f(0) = 0. But f(g) + f(g) + ... + f(g) n times = n*k. But in the codomain ℤ_n, n*k ≡ 0 mod n, which is always true. Therefore, any choice of k in ℤ_n will satisfy this condition. Therefore, every map defined by f(g) = k for some k in ℤ_n will extend to a homomorphism. Hence, the number of homomorphisms is equal to the number of elements in ℤ_n, which is n.

Wait, but that can't be right. Wait, for example, if n is prime, say 5, then the number of homomorphisms from ℤ_5 to ℤ_5 would be 5? That sounds correct. Each homomorphism is multiplication by some element k in ℤ_5. Let's check with n=2. Homomorphisms from ℤ_2 to ℤ_2. There are two: the zero map and the identity map. But according to this, n=2 would have 2 homomorphisms, which is correct. For n=3, three homomorphisms: 0, 1, 2. Each defined by multiplication. So that seems right.

Wait, but another thought: when you have a cyclic group, the number of homomorphisms from ℤ_n to ℤ_n is n, since Hom(ℤ_n, ℤ_n) is isomorphic to ℤ_n as a group under addition, but as a set, it's of order n. So yes, each homomorphism is multiplication by some k mod n, and there are n choices.

Therefore, the homomorphisms are exactly the maps f_k: ℤ_n → ℤ_n defined by f_k(x) = kx mod n, where k is in {0, 1, 2, ..., n-1}. Hence, there are n homomorphisms.

Wait, but wait another second. Let me test this with an example. Take n=4. Then, Hom(ℤ_4, ℤ_4) should have 4 elements. Let's list them:

k=0: The zero map, sending every element to 0.

k=1: The identity map, each x maps to x.

k=2: Each x maps to 2x mod 4. So 0→0, 1→2, 2→0, 3→2.

k=3: Each x maps to 3x mod 4. So 0→0, 1→3, 2→2, 3→1.

Are these all homomorphisms? Let's check for k=3. For example, f(1 + 1) = f(2) = 2, and f(1) + f(1) = 3 + 3 = 6 ≡ 2 mod 4. So that works. Similarly, f(1 + 2) = f(3) = 1, and f(1) + f(2) = 3 + 2 = 5 ≡ 1 mod 4. So that's correct. So for each k, the map f_k is a homomorphism, and there are n of them. So the answer should be that there are n homomorphisms, each given by multiplication by an element of ℤ_n.

But just to make sure, let's check n=1. Then ℤ_1 has only the trivial group. There's only one homomorphism, which is the identity. So n=1 gives 1 homomorphism, which matches.

Alternatively, think in terms of group theory. The group Hom(ℤ_n, ℤ_n) is isomorphic to ℤ_n itself because ℤ_n is cyclic. The automorphism group (which is the group of invertible homomorphisms) is Aut(ℤ_n) ≅ ℤ_n^×, the multiplicative group of units mod n, which has φ(n) elements. But here we are considering all homomorphisms, not just the invertible ones. So Hom(ℤ_n, ℤ_n) is isomorphic to ℤ_n as a group under addition, so its order is n. Therefore, the number is n.

Therefore, the conclusion is there are n homomorphisms from ℤ_n to ℤ_n, each defined by multiplication by a fixed element k in ℤ_n.

Phase <#sep#> group under addition, so its order is n. Therefore, the number is n.

Therefore, the conclusion is there are n homomorphisms from ℤ_n to ℤ_n, each defined by multiplication by a fixed element k in ℤ_n.
</think>To determine the number of homomorphisms from \(\mathbb{Z}_n\) to \(\mathbb{Z}_n\), we start by recalling the properties of group homomorphisms and the structure of \(\mathbb{Z}_n\).

1. **Definition of Homomorphism:**
   A homomorphism \(f: \mathbb{Z}_n \to \mathbb{Z}_n\) must satisfy the property \(f(a + b) = f(a) + f(b)\) for all \(a, b \in \mathbb{Z}_n\).

2. **Cyclic Group Property:**
   Since \(\mathbb{Z}_n\) is a cyclic group generated by \(1 \mod n\), a homomorphism \(f\) is completely determined by the image of the generator \(1\). Specifically, if \(f(1) = k\), then for any integer \(m\),
   \[
   f(m) = f(1 + 1 + \cdots + 1) = f(1) + f(1) + \cdots + f(1) = m \cdot f(1) = mk \mod n.
   \]
   Thus, the homomorphism \(f\) is given by \(f(m) = km \mod n\).

3. **Verification of Homomorphism:**
   We need to verify that \(f\) defined by \(f(m) = km \mod n\) is indeed a homomorphism. For any \(a, b \in \mathbb{Z}_n\),
   \[
   f(a + b) = k(a + b) \mod n = (ka + kb) \mod n = f(a) + f(b).
   \]
   This shows that \(f\) satisfies the homomorphism property.

4. **Counting Homomorphisms:**
   Since \(f\) is determined by the choice of \(k\), and \(k\) can be any element in \(\mathbb{Z}_n\), there are exactly \(n\) possible choices for \(k\). Therefore, there are \(n\) distinct homomorphisms from \(\mathbb{Z}_n\) to \(\mathbb{Z}_n\).

5. **Explicit Homomorphisms:**
   The homomorphisms are given by the maps \(f_k: \mathbb{Z}_n \to \mathbb{Z}_n\) defined by
   \[
   f_k(x) = kx \mod n
   \]
   for \(k = 0, 1, 2, \ldots, n-1\).

Thus, the number of homomorphisms from \(\mathbb{Z}_n\) to \(\mathbb{Z}_n\) is \(\boxed{n}\). Each homomorphism is given by multiplication by a fixed element \(k\) in \(\mathbb{Z}_n\).<#sep#> is 1 second (180度转).

## Phase 1. READING: Understanding the Problem



tool: Your thought has been logged.

assistant: ### 1.1-1.3 Problem Analysis

The issue is with a **SCIM (System for Cross-domain Identity Management) filter parser** that fails when processing queries with multiple consecutive "or" or "and" operators of the same type.

**Key Technical Details:**
- **Error**: `scim2_filter_parser.parser.SCIMParserError`
- **Error Location**: Token parsing at specific position (e.g., `Token(type='OR', value='or', lineno=1, index=52)`)
- **Module**: `scim2_filter_parser.parser`
- **Failing Cases**: Multiple consecutive same-type operators (all "or" or all "and")
- **Working Case**: Mixed operators ("and" followed by "or")

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Parse query with 3+ consecutive "or" operators: `"displayName co \"username\" or nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""`
2. Parse query with 3+ consecutive "and" operators: `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" and userName co \"username\""`
3. Both should fail with `SCIMParserError`
4. Parse query with mixed operators: `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""` - this should work

### 1.5 Best Practices
- Ensure proper operator precedence handling
- Support left-to-right evaluation of same-type operators
- Maintain backward compatibility with existing working queries
- Follow SCIM specification for filter syntax

    19	
    20	SCIM 2.0 defines queries that look like this::
    21	
    22	    'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
    23	
    24	These can be hard to work with and covert into SQL to run against a database.
    25	
    26	That's where SCIM 2.0 Filter Parser (SFP) can help.
    27	
    28	SFP is broken up into four modules, each handling a different part of
    29	translating a SCIM call into a SQL query.
    30	
    31	The first step is tokenization or lexical analysis where the filter query

    96	The third step is to transpile this AST into a language of our choice.
    97	The above query is transpiled to SQL below.
    98

   104	    {0: 'work', 1: '%@example.com%', 2: 'xmpp', 3: '%@foo.com%'}
   105	
   106	The fourth step is to take what is a segment of a SQL WHERE clause and complete
   107	the rest of the SQL query.
   108	
   109	::
   110	
   111	    sfp-query 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'

   120	        WHERE ((emails.type = work) AND (emails.value LIKE %@example.com%)) OR ((ims.type = xmpp) AND (ims.value LIKE %@foo.com%));
   121	
   122	Please note that SFP does not build SQL queries with parameters pre-injected.
   123	That would create a SQL injection attack vulnerability. Instead a ``SQLQuery``
   124	object is created and can be forced to display itself as seen above
   125	by ``print`` ing the query object.
   126	
   127	Installation
   128	------------
   129	::
   130	
   131	    pip install scim2-filter-parser
   132	
   133	Use
   134	---
   135	
   136	Although command line shims are provided, the library is intended to be used
   137	programmatically. Users of the library should instantiate the
   138	``scim2_filter_parser.queries.SQLQuery`` class with an attribute map and optionally
   139	any joins necessary to make all required fields accessible in the query.
   140	
   141	For example, if user information is stored in the ``users`` table and email
   142	information is stored in a different table ``emails``, then the attribute map
   143	and the joins might be defined as so::
   144	
   145	    from scim2_filter_parser.queries import SQLQuery
   146	
   147	    attr_map = {
   148	        ('userName', None, None): 'users.username',
   149	        ('name', 'familyName', None): 'users.family_name',
   150	        ('meta', 'lastModified', None): 'users.update_ts',
   151	        ('emails', None, None): 'emails.address',
   152	        ('emails', 'value', None): 'emails.address',
   153	    }
   154	
   155	    joins = (
   156	        'LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id',
   157	    )
   158	
   159	    filter_ = 'name.familyName co "Simpson" or emails.value eq "lisa@example.com"'
   160	
   161	    q = SQLQuery(filter_, 'users', attr_map, joins)
   162	
   163	    q.sql # Will be...
   164	
   165	    SELECT DISTINCT users.*
   166	    FROM users
   167	    LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id
   168	    WHERE (users.family_name LIKE %s) OR (emails.address = %s);
   169	
   170	    q.params # Will be...
   171	
   172	    ['%Simpson%', 'lisa@example.com']
   173	
   174	The attribute_map (``attr_map``) is a mapping of SCIM attribute, subattribute,
   175	and schema uri to a table field. You will need to customize this to your
   176	particular database schema.

4026/122=33. Then, 33 -122 +1=-88. Negative. So no. Hence, all divisors larger than 61 result in negative n, so<#sep#>human: Nezzar loves the game osu!.

osu! is played on beatmaps, which can be seen as an array consisting of distinct points on a plane. A beatmap is called nice if for any three consecutive points $A,B,C$ listed in order, the angle between these three points, centered at $B$, is strictly less than $90$ degrees.

Points $A,B,C$ on the left have angle less than $90$ degrees, so they can be three consecutive points of a nice beatmap; Points $A',B',C'$ on the right have angle greater or equal to $90$ degrees, so they cannot be three consecutive points of a nice beatmap.

Now Nezzar has a beatmap of $n$ distinct points $A_1,A_2,\ldots,A_n$. Nezzar would like to reorder these $n$ points so that the resulting beatmap is nice.

Formally, you are required to find a permutation $p_1,p_2,\ldots,p_n$ of integers from $1$ to $n$, such that beatmap $A_{p_1},A_{p_2},\ldots,A_{p_n}$ is nice. If it is impossible, you should determine it.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($3 \le n \le 5000$).

Then $n$ lines follow, $i$-th of them contains two integers $x_i$, $y_i$ ($-10^9 \le x_i, y_i \le 10^9$) — coordinates of point $A_i$.

It is guaranteed that all points are distinct.


-----Output-----

If there is no solution, print $-1$.

Otherwise, print $n$ integers, representing a valid permutation $p$.

If there are multiple possible answers, you can print any.


-----Examples-----

Input
5
0 0
5 0
4 2
2 1
3 0
Output
1 2 5 3 4


-----Note-----

Here is the illustration for the first test:

Please note that the angle between $A_1$, $A_2$ and $A_5$, centered at $A_2$, is treated as $0$ degrees. However, angle between $A_1$, $A_5$ and $A_2$, centered at $A_5$, is treated as $180$ degrees.

   174	The attribute_map (``attr_map``) is a mapping of SCIM attribute, subattribute,
   175	and schema uri to a table field. You will need to customize this to your
   176	particular database schema.
   177	
   178	The ``SQLQuery.sql`` method returns SQL that can be used as the first
   179	argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()`` with your favorite DB engine.
   180	If you are using a database that requires a replacement character other than '%s',
   181	then you can subclass the ``SQLQuery`` class and override the ``placeholder`` class
   182	level variable. See the query module and unit tests for an example of this subclassing
   183	with SQLite.
   184	
   185	The ``SQLQuery.params`` method returns a list of items that can be used as the
   186	second argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()``.
   187	
   188	Django
   189	------
   190	
   191	If you would like to produce a `Django Q`_ object instead of a raw SQL query, you can pass
   192	a SCIM filter query and attribute map to the ``get_query`` function from the module
   193	``scim2_filter_parser.transpilers.django_q_object``. For example::
   194	
   195	    get_query(scim_query: str, attr_map: Mapping)
   196	
   197	This Q object can then be passed to a Django filter query like so::
   198	
   199	    query = get_query(scim_query, attr_map)
   200	    User.objects.filter(query)
   201	
   202	Please note that you will need to install the Django Query extra like for this feature to be available::
   203	
   204	    pip install scim2-filter-parser[django-query]
   205	
   206	.. _`Django Q`: https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/3.1/topics/db/queries/#complex-lookups-with-q-objects

   211	SFP is pretty fast. Check out the speed_test.py script for details on the long and short
   212	filter queries tested. SFP transpiled a short filter query into SQL in under 54 microseconds.
   213	For a longer query, SFP only took 273 microseconds.

</think>To find the maximum possible value of \( k \) such that 2013 can be written as the sum of \( k \) consecutive positive integers, we start with the formula for the sum of \( k \) consecutive integers starting from \( n \):

\[
2013 = \frac{k}{2} \left[ 2n + (k - 1) \right]
\]

Multiplying both sides by 2 to eliminate the fraction, we get:

\[
4026 = k(2n + k - 1)
\]

\[
4026 = 2 \times 3 \times 11 \times 61
\]

The divisors of 4026 are:

\[

\]

The fourth point needs to have the second to third step vector with the third to fourth step vector's angle also less than 90, and so on.

This resembles the problem of finding a path through all points where each turn is acute. To do this, a greedy algorithm might work as follows:

1. Choose a starting point. (Could choose one with the most flexibility?)

2. Among remaining points, choose the next point as one that forms the smallest angle with the previous direction. 

3. Continue until all points are used.

But the problem is how to efficiently pick the next point at each step. 

Selection might be possible with a priority queue, where you always pick the closest or easiest path, but ensuring that the turn is valid. However, this might not always work for some cases. 

This greedy approach might not always yield a solution even if one exists, but perhaps the constraints can be met by choosing the next point in a way that greedily extends the path. 

Alternatively, maybe the following approach could work:

Choose any starting point, then any second point. Then for each subsequent point, pick one such that the new direction forms an acute angle with the previous one, continuing until all points are used.

But the problem is how to efficiently find such points. 

Wait, for n=5000, this requires doing an O(n) search each step, which would be O(n^2) time. For 5000 that's 25,000,000 steps, which might be manageable with optimizations (like pruning already visited points in a bitmask, but that's impossible since n is large). 

Wait but with n up to 5000, O(n^2) is 25 million, but even squared can be high. Maybe with smart processing, it could work.

Alternatively, perhaps the following observation: that the required path can be constructed by always extending at one end (e.g., adding to the end or the beginning) as long as the angle is okay. So keeping the path as a list, and at each step adding either end the next point. 

Wait that's the Hamilton path idea known in some problems where you add to the end if the angle is acute.

2. Maintain a set of remaining points. Also, track the current direction vectors for the start and end of the path.

     1	'''
     2	This file defines the parser class that is used to parse a given SCIM query.
     3	
     4	See https://tools.ietf.org/html/rfc7644#section-3.4.2.2 for more details
     5	
     6	::
     7	
     8	    SCIM filters MUST conform to the following ABNF [RFC5234] rules as
     9	    specified below:
    10	
    11	      FILTER    = attrExp / logExp / valuePath / *1"not" "(" FILTER ")"
    12	
    13	      valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
    14	                  ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
    15	
    16	      valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
    17	
    18	      attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
    19	                  (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
    20	
    21	      logExp    = FILTER SP ("and" / "or") SP FILTER
    22	
    23	      compValue = false / null / true / number / string
    24	                  ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
    25	
    26	      compareOp = "eq" / "ne" / "co" /
    27	                         "sw" / "ew" /
    28	                         "gt" / "lt" /
    29	                         "ge" / "le"
    30	
    31	      attrPath  = [URI ":"] ATTRNAME *1subAttr
    32	                  ; SCIM attribute name
    33	                  ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
    34	
    35	      ATTRNAME  = ALPHA *(nameChar)
    36	
    37	      nameChar  = "-" / "_" / DIGIT / ALPHA
    38	
    39	      subAttr   = "." ATTRNAME
    40	                  ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
    41	
    42	                Figure 1: ABNF Specification of SCIM Filters
    43	
    44	    In the above ABNF rules, the "compValue" (comparison value) rule is
    45	    built on JSON Data Interchange format ABNF rules as specified in
    46	    [RFC7159], "DIGIT" and "ALPHA" are defined per Appendix B.1 of
    47	    [RFC5234], and "URI" is defined per Appendix A of [RFC3986].
    48	
    49	    Filters MUST be evaluated using the following order of operations, in
    50	    order of precedence:
    51	
    52	    1.  Grouping operators
    53	
    54	    2.  Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and",
    55	        which takes precedence over "or"
    56	
    57	    3.  Attribute operators
    58	
    59	    If the specified attribute in a filter expression is a multi-valued
    60	    attribute, the filter matches if any of the values of the specified
    61	    attribute match the specified criterion; e.g., if a User has multiple
    62	    "emails" values, only one has to match for the entire User to match.
    63	    For complex attributes, a fully qualified sub-attribute MUST be
    64	    specified using standard attribute notation (Section 3.10).  For
    65	    example, to filter by userName, the parameter value is "userName".
    66	    To filter by first name, the parameter value is "name.givenName".
    67	
    68	    When applying a comparison (e.g., "eq") or presence filter (e.g.,
    69	    "pr") to a defaulted attribute, the service provider SHALL use the
    70	    value that was returned to the client that last created or modified
    71	    the attribute.
    72	
    73	    Providers MAY support additional filter operations if they choose.
    74	    Providers MUST decline to filter results if the specified filter
    75	    operation is not recognized and return an HTTP 400 error with a
    76	    "scimType" error of "invalidFilter" and an appropriate human-readable
    77	    response as per Section 3.12.  For example, if a client specified an
    78	    unsupported operator named 'regex', the service provider should
    79	    specify an error response description identifying the client error,
    80	    e.g., 'The operator 'regex' is not supported.'
    81	
    82	    When comparing attributes of type String, the case sensitivity for
    83	    String type attributes SHALL be determined by the attribute's

    85

    95	class SCIMParserError(Exception):
    96	    pass
    97	
    98	
    99	class SCIMParser(Parser):
   100	    # debugfile = 'debug.log'
   101	
   102	    # Same token set as defined in the lexer
   103	    tokens = lexer.SCIMLexer.tokens
   104	
   105	    # Operator precedence table.
   106	    #   Filters MUST be evaluated using the following order of operations, in
   107	    #   order of precedence:
   108	    #   1.  Grouping operators
   109	    #   2.  Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and",
   110	    #       which takes precedence over "or"
   111	    #   3.  Attribute operators
   112	    precedence = (
   113	        ('nonassoc', OR),  # noqa F821
   114	        ('nonassoc', AND), # noqa F821
   115	        ('nonassoc', NOT), # noqa F821
   116	    )
   117	
   118	    # FILTER    = attrExp / logExp / valuePath / *1"not" "(" FILTER ")"
   119	    #                                           ; 0 or 1 "not"s
   120	    @_('attr_exp')  # noqa F821
   121	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   122	        return ast.Filter(p.attr_exp, False, None)
   123	
   124	    @_('log_exp')  # noqa F821
   125	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   126	        return ast.Filter(p.log_exp, False, None)
   127	
   128	    @_('value_path')  # noqa F821
   129	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   130	        return ast.Filter(p.value_path, False, None)
   131	
   132	    @_('LPAREN filter RPAREN', # noqa F821
   133	       'NOT LPAREN filter RPAREN')
   134	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   135	        negate = p[0].lower() == 'not'
   136	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, negate, None)
   137	
   138	    # valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
   139	    #            ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
   140	    # valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
   141	    #                               ; 0 or 1 "not"s
   142	    #
   143	    # Since valFilter and Filter have the same structure, we have to
   144	    # parse valFilter as a Filter. Else we'll run into reduce/reduce conflicts.
   145	    #
   146	    # attr_path here acts like a namespace for the filter operation inside
   147	    # the brackets. Thus we need to distribute the namespace to the leaf nodes
   148	    # of the filter node. We will attach attr_path to the filter and use
   149	    # it in the transpiler.
   150	    @_('attr_path LBRACKET filter RBRACKET') # noqa F821
   151	    def value_path(self, p):
   152	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, False, p.attr_path)
   153	
   154	    # attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
   155	    #             (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
   156	    @_('attr_path PR', # noqa F821
   157	       'attr_path EQ comp_value',
   158	       'attr_path NE comp_value',
   159	       'attr_path CO comp_value',
   160	       'attr_path SW comp_value',
   161	       'attr_path EW comp_value',
   162	       'attr_path GT comp_value',
   163	       'attr_path LT comp_value',
   164	       'attr_path GE comp_value',
   165	       'attr_path LE comp_value')
   166	    def attr_exp(self, p):
   167	        comp_value = p.comp_value if len(p) == 3 else None
   168	        return ast.AttrExpr(p[1], p.attr_path, comp_value)
   169	
   170	    # logExp    = FILTER SP ("and" / "or") SP FILTER
   171	    @_('filter OR filter', # noqa F821
   172	       'filter AND filter')
   173	    def log_exp(self, p):
   174	        return ast.LogExpr(p[1], p.filter0, p.filter1)
   175	
   176	    # compValue = false / null / true / number / string
   177	    #            ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
   178	    @_('FALSE', # noqa F821
   179	       'NULL',
   180	       'TRUE',
   181	       'NUMBER',
   182	       'COMP_VALUE')
   183	    def comp_value(self, p):
   184	        return ast.CompValue(p[0])
   185	
   186	    # attrPath  = [URI ":"] ATTRNAME *1subAttr
   187	    #             ; SCIM attribute name
   188	    #             ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
   189	    @_('ATTRNAME', # noqa F821
   190	       'ATTRNAME sub_attr',
   191	       'SCHEMA_URI ATTRNAME',
   192	       'SCHEMA_URI ATTRNAME sub_attr',
   193	       # Next clause is not in spec but allows us to handle things
   194	       # like 'members[value eq "6784"] eq ""' which are helpful for
   195	       # AttrPath parsing for PATCH calls.
   196	       'value_path',
   197	       # Not sure this last clause is in the ABNF spec
   198	       # but Azure/Microsoft uses it so let's be prepared.
   199	       'value_path sub_attr')
   200	    def attr_path(self, p):
   201	        if len(p) == 1:
   202	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   203	
   204	        elif len(p) == 2 and isinstance(p[0], ast.Filter) and isinstance(p[1], ast.SubAttr):
   205	            sub_attr = p[0].namespace.sub_attr
   206	            if sub_attr is not None:
   207	                raise SCIMParserError(f'Parsing error at: {p}')
   208	
   209	            # For easier transpiling, convert complex queries like so:
   210	            # emails[type eq "Primary"].value -> emails.value[type eq "Primary"]
   211	            p[0].namespace.sub_attr = p[1]
   212	
   213	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   214	
   215	        elif len(p) == 2 and isinstance(p[1], ast.SubAttr):
   216	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], p[1], None)
   217	
   218	        elif len(p) == 2:
   219	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], None, p[0])
   220	
   221	        else:
   222	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], p[2], p[0])
   223	
   224	    # subAttr   = "." ATTRNAME
   225	    #             ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
   226	    @_('SUBATTR') # noqa F821
   227	    def sub_attr(self, p):
   228	        return ast.SubAttr(p[0])
   229	
   230	    def error(self, p):
   231	        raise SCIMParserError(f'Parsing error at: {p}')
   232

Wait the vector that's used for checking adding to front is:

The vector from the current first point (path[0]) to the second (path[1}) is vector (dx, dy).

When adding T before path[0], then the condition is that vector from T to path[0] and from path[0] to path[1] have to form an angle less than 90. 

The vectors for the angle at path[0] are (T - path[0]), and (path[1]-path[0]). 

Thus their dot product must be positive:

 ( (T.x - a.x) )*(dx) + ( (T.y -a.y)*dy ) >0, where a is path[0].

For the FRONT:

dx is the delta between the first and second point: dx=path[1].x - path[0].x, dy=path[1].y - path[0].y.

 Similarly for the end condition, the vectors involve the last two points.

Thus, perhaps to speed up, we can precompute the vectors:

Current path is a list, so we can have pointers to the first two and last two points each iteration.

vector front_dir: (pos1.x - pos0.x, pos1.y - pos0.y).

Wait no. Wait the dot product between vectors (from T to pos0) and (pos1 to pos0) ?

Wait the vectors at path[0], which is T's position is inserted before current path.

Wait let me re-express:

 The angle is at pos0 (the first point of current path after insertion), between T, pos0, and pos1.

 The vectors are 

 Vector from pos0 to T → (T - pos0)

 Vector from pos0 to pos1 → (pos1 - pos0).

Wait the angle between those vectors must be less than 90 degrees, so their dot product must be >0.

The dot product is (T.x - pos0.x)*(pos1.x - pos0.x) + (T.y - pos0.y)*( pos1.y - pos0.y ) > 0.

Which is the same as ( (T.x - pos0.x)*dx ) + ... etc.

Ah right. So for each candidate T to add to front: compute that value.

Similarly for the end condition.

Thus, the code can precompute the front and end vectors each iteration, then loop through the remaining points, and compute the required values.

Thus, to save time:

Precompute for each iteration:

front_dx = path[1].x - path[0].x

front_dy = path[1].y - path[0].y

end_dx = path[-2].x - path[-1].x   # previous to last step.

end_dy = path[-2].y - path[-1].y

Then, when checking a candidate T, you can compute the front condition, end condition based on these precomputed delta values.

This reduces computation time.

Thus, in each step, the loops can be manageable.

</think>To solve the equation \( x^4 + 10x^2 + 2^y = z^2 \) for natural numbers \( x, y, z \), we will systematically test small values of \( x \) and check if the resulting expression can be a perfect square.

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Testing \( x = 2 \):**
   - Substitute \( x = 2 \) into the equation:
     \[
     2^4 + 10 \cdot 2^2 + 2^y = z^2
     \]
   - Simplify the expression:
     \[
     16 + 40 + 2^y = z^2 \implies 56 + 2^y = z^2
     \]
   - We need \( 56 + 2^y \) to be a perfect square. Testing small values of \( y \):
     - For \( y = 3 \):
       \[
       56 + 2^3 = 56 + 8 = 64 \implies z^2 = 64 \implies z = 8
       \]
     - Thus, \( (x, y, z) = (2, 3, 8) \) is a solution.

2. **Testing other values of \( x \):**
   - For \( x = 1 \):
     \[
     1^4 + 10 \cdot 1^2 + 2^y = z^2 \implies 1 + 10 + 2^y = z^2 \implies 11 + 2^y = z^2
     \]
     - Testing small values of \( y \):
       - For \( y = 0 \): \( 11 + 2^0 = 12 \), not a perfect square.
       - For \( y = 1 \): \( 11 + 2^1 = 13 \), not a perfect square.

   found = False

   if not found:

       return -1 (no solution)

Thus, here we break at the first T found that can be added. 

This reduces the inner loop to break early once a candidate is found. Thus, in best case, each step takes O(1) time (if the first T in the remaining list (e.g., list being in order) is a candidate). But worst case, it takes O(r) time where r is remaining nodes, but maybe on average better.

Okay, so I have this limit to evaluate: the limit as x approaches infinity of the square root of x times the difference between the cube root of (x plus 1) and the cube root of (x minus 1). Hmm, let me write that down again to make sure I have it right.

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \sqrt{x} \left( \sqrt[3]{x+1} - \sqrt[3]{x-1} \right)
\]

Alright, so it's the product of sqrt(x) and the difference of two cube roots. Since x is going to infinity, both x+1 and x-1 are going to be very close to x when x is large. But their cube roots might differ by a small amount, and multiplying by sqrt(x) could amplify that difference. The question is whether this product approaches a finite limit, zero, or infinity.

I remember that when dealing with limits involving roots, especially differences of roots, expanding them using binomial approximations or using the conjugate can be helpful. But cube roots are a bit trickier than square roots. Let me think.

For square roots, we often multiply by the conjugate to rationalize. For cube roots, maybe we can use the formula for a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2). So if I let a = cube root of (x+1) and b = cube root of (x-1), then a^3 - b^3 = (x+1) - (x-1) = 2. Therefore, a - b = 2 / (a^2 + ab + b^2). So maybe I can express the difference of the cube roots as 2 divided by the sum of their squares and their product. Then multiply by sqrt(x). Let's try that.

Let me set a = (x + 1)^{1/3} and b = (x - 1)^{1/3}. Then, as I said, a - b = 2 / (a^2 + ab + b^2). Therefore, the original expression becomes sqrt(x) * 2 / (a^2 + ab + b^2). So:

sqrt(x) * 2 / [ ( (x + 1)^{2/3} + (x + 1)^{1/3}(x - 1)^{1/3} + (x - 1)^{2/3} ) ]

Hmm, okay. So the denominator is a sum of three terms. Each of these terms is something raised to the 2/3 power or the product of two terms raised to the 1/3 power. Maybe I can factor out x from each term in the cube roots?

Let's try to approximate these terms for large x. Since x is approaching infinity, x + 1 ≈ x and x - 1 ≈ x. So maybe I can write (x + 1)^{1/3} ≈ x^{1/3}(1 + 1/x)^{1/3} and similarly for (x - 1)^{1/3} ≈ x^{1/3}(1 - 1/x)^{1/3}. Then, using the binomial approximation for (1 + ε)^k ≈ 1 + kε when ε is small.

Let me expand each term:

First, (x + 1)^{1/3} = x^{1/3}(1 + 1/x)^{1/3} ≈ x^{1/3}(1 + (1/3)(1/x) - (1/9)(1/x^2) + ... )

Similarly, (x - 1)^{1/3} = x^{1/3}(1 - 1/x)^{1/3} ≈ x^{1/3}(1 - (1/3)(1/x) - (1/9)(1/x^2) + ... )

Therefore, their difference, (x + 1)^{1/3} - (x - 1)^{1/3} ≈ x^{1/3}[ (1 + 1/(3x)) - (1 - 1/(3x)) ] = x^{1/3}(2/(3x)) = 2/(3x^{2/3})

Wait, but the original expression is sqrt(x) times this difference. So sqrt(x) times 2/(3x^{2/3}) = 2/(3x^{2/3 - 1/2}) = 2/(3x^{1/6}) ) → 0 as x → ∞. But that would imply the limit is zero. But is that right?

Wait, but maybe the approximation is too crude? Because the higher-order terms might contribute when multiplied by sqrt(x). Let's check this again.

Alternatively, maybe I need to be more precise with the expansion. Let me denote t = 1/x, so as x → ∞, t → 0. Then, (x + 1)^{1/3} = (x(1 + t))^{1/3} = x^{1/3}(1 + t)^{1/3} ≈ x^{1/3}(1 + (1/3)t - (1/9)t^2 + ... )

Similarly, (x - 1)^{1/3} = x^{1/3}(1 - t)^{1/3} ≈ x^{1/3}(1 - (1/3)t - (1/9)t^2 + ... )

Subtracting these two, we get:

x^{1/3}[ (1 + (1/3)t - (1/9)t^2) - (1 - (1/3)t - (1/9)t^2) ) ] = x^{1/3}[ (2/3)t - (2/9)t^2 + ... ] = x^{1/3}[ (2/3)(1/x) - (2/9)(1/x^2) + ... ]

So the difference (x + 1)^{1/3} - (x - 1)^{1/3} ≈ (2/3)x^{-2/3} - (2/9)x^{-5/3} + ...

Multiplying this by sqrt(x) = x^{1/2}:

sqrt(x) * [ (2/3)x^{-2/3} - (2/9)x^{-5/3} + ... ] = (2/3)x^{1/2 - 2/3} - (2/9)x^{1/2 - 5/3} + ... = (2/3)x^{-1/6} - (2/9)x^{-7/6} + ...

As x approaches infinity, x^{-1/6} approaches 0 and x^{-7/6} approaches 0 even faster. Therefore, the entire expression tends to 0. So according to this approximation, the limit is zero. But wait, I recall that sometimes when you have differences of roots multiplied by a certain power, the limit can be a finite number. Maybe my approximation is missing something?

Alternatively, maybe using the expansion with more terms? Let me check.

Wait, but perhaps instead of expanding each cube root separately, I should apply the mean value theorem. For a function f(t) = t^{1/3}, the difference f(x+1) - f(x-1) can be approximated by f'(c)(2), where c is between x - 1 and x + 1. Then, by the mean value theorem, there exists c in (x - 1, x + 1) such that:

f(x + 1) - f(x - 1) = f'(c) * 2

So f'(c) = (1/3)c^{-2/3}

Therefore, the difference is 2/(3c^{2/3})

Then, the original expression is sqrt(x) * 2/(3c^{2/3})

Since c is between x - 1 and x + 1, as x approaches infinity, c is approximately x. Therefore, c^{2/3} ≈ x^{2/3}

Therefore, sqrt(x) * 2/(3x^{2/3}) = (2/3) x^{1/2 - 2/3} = (2/3) x^{-1/6} → 0 as x → ∞

So this also suggests the limit is zero.

But maybe there's a mistake here? Because sometimes when using approximations, if the higher order terms are significant when multiplied by another term, but in this case, both methods give zero.<#sep#>:

Since a = (x + 1)^{1/3}, b = (x - 1)^{1/3}

Similarly, b^2 = (x - 1)^{2/3} ≈ x^{2/3}(1 - (2/3)(1/x) - (2/9)(1/x^2) + ... )

ab = (x + 1)^{1/3}(x - 1)^{1/3} = [ (x + 1)(x - 1) ]^{1/3} = (x^2 - 1)^{1/3} = x^{2/3}(1 - 1/x^2)^{1/3} ≈ x^{2/3}(1 - (1/3)(1/x^2) + ... )

ab ≈ x^{2/3}(1 - (1/(3x^2)))

a^2 + ab + b^2 ≈ x^{2/3}[ (1 + 2/(3x) - 2/(9x^2)) + (1 - 2/(3x) - 2/(9x^2)) + (1 - 1/(3x^2)) ]

-2/(9x^2) -2/(9x^2) -1/(3x^2) = (-4/9 - 3/9)/x^2 = (-7/9)/x^2

    17	Description
    18	-----------
    19	
    20	SCIM 2.0 defines queries that look like this::
    21	
    22	    'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
    23	
    24	These can be hard to work with and covert into SQL to run against a database.
    25	
    26	That's where SCIM 2.0 Filter Parser (SFP) can help.
    27	
    28	SFP is broken up into four modules, each handling a different part of
    29	translating a SCIM call into a SQL query.
    30	
    31	The first step is tokenization or lexical analysis where the filter query
    32	is broken down into many tokens that make it up.
    33	
    34	::
    35	
    36	    sfp-lexer 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
    37	
    38	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='emails', lineno=1, index=0)
    39	    Token(type='LBRACKET', value='[', lineno=1, index=6)
    40	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='type', lineno=1, index=7)
    41	    Token(type='EQ', value='eq', lineno=1, index=12)
    42	    Token(type='COMP_VALUE', value='work', lineno=1, index=15)
    43	    Token(type='AND', value='and', lineno=1, index=22)
    44	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='value', lineno=1, index=26)
    45	    Token(type='CO', value='co', lineno=1, index=32)
    46	    Token(type='COMP_VALUE', value='@example.com', lineno=1, index=35)
    47	    Token(type='RBRACKET', value=']', lineno=1, index=49)
    48	    Token(type='OR', value='or', lineno=1, index=51)
    49	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='ims', lineno=1, index=54)
    50	    Token(type='LBRACKET', value='[', lineno=1, index=57)
    51	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='type', lineno=1, index=58)
    52	    Token(type='EQ', value='eq', lineno=1, index=63)
    53	    Token(type='COMP_VALUE', value='xmpp', lineno=1, index=66)
    54	    Token(type='AND', value='and', lineno=1, index=73)
    55	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='value', lineno=1, index=77)
    56	    Token(type='CO', value='co', lineno=1, index=83)
    57	    Token(type='COMP_VALUE', value='@foo.com', lineno=1, index=86)
    58	    Token(type='RBRACKET', value=']', lineno=1, index=96)
    59	
    60	
    61	The second step is to convert that series of tokens into a abstract syntax tree.
    62	
    63	::
    64	
    65	    sfp-parser 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'

    95	
    96	The third step is to transpile this AST into a language of our choice.
    97	The above query is transpiled to SQL below.
    98	
    99	::
   100	
   101	    sfp-transpiler 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
   102	
   103	    ((emails.type = {0}) AND (emails.value LIKE {1})) OR ((ims.type = {2}) AND (ims.value LIKE {3}))
   104	    {0: 'work', 1: '%@example.com%', 2: 'xmpp', 3: '%@foo.com%'}
   105	
   106	The fourth step is to take what is a segment of a SQL WHERE clause and complete
   107	the rest of the SQL query.
   108	
   109	::
   110	
   111	    sfp-query 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
   112	
   113	    >>> DO NOT USE THIS OUTPUT DIRECTLY
   114	    >>> SQL INJECTION ATTACK RISK
   115	    >>> SQL PREVIEW:
   116	        SELECT DISTINCT users.*
   117	        FROM users
   118	        LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id
   119	        LEFT JOIN schemas ON schemas.user_id = users.id
   120	        WHERE ((emails.type = work) AND (emails.value LIKE %@example.com%)) OR ((ims.type = xmpp) AND (ims.value LIKE %@foo.com%));
   121	
   122	Please note that SFP does not build SQL queries with parameters pre-injected.
   123	That would create a SQL injection attack vulnerability. Instead a ``SQLQuery``
   124	object is created and can be forced to display itself as seen above
   125	by ``print`` ing the query object.
   126	
   127	Installation
   128	------------
   129	::
   130	
   131	    pip install scim2-filter-parser
   132	
   133	Use
   134	---
   135	
   136	Although command line shims are provided, the library is intended to be used
   137	programmatically. Users of the library should instantiate the
   138	``scim2_filter_parser.queries.SQLQuery`` class with an attribute map and optionally
   139	any joins necessary to make all required fields accessible in the query.
   140	
   141	For example, if user information is stored in the ``users`` table and email
   142	information is stored in a different table ``emails``, then the attribute map
   143	and the joins might be defined as so::
   144	
   145	    from scim2_filter_parser.queries import SQLQuery
   146	
   147	    attr_map = {
   148	        ('userName', None, None): 'users.username',
   149	        ('name', 'familyName', None): 'users.family_name',
   150	        ('meta', 'lastModified', None): 'users.update_ts',
   151	        ('emails', None, None): 'emails.address',
   152	        ('emails', 'value', None): 'emails.address',
   153	    }
   154	
   155	    joins = (
   156	        'LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id',
   157	    )
   158	
   159	    filter_ = 'name.familyName co "Simpson" or emails.value eq "lisa@example.com"'
   160	
   161	    q = SQLQuery(filter_, 'users', attr_map, joins)
   162	
   163	    q.sql # Will be...
   164	
   165	    SELECT DISTINCT users.*
   166	    FROM users
   167	    LEFT JOIN emails ON emails.user_id = users.id
   168	    WHERE (users.family_name LIKE %s) OR (emails.address = %s);
   169	
   170	    q.params # Will be...
   171	
   172	    ['%Simpson%', 'lisa@example.com']
   173	
   174	The attribute_map (``attr_map``) is a mapping of SCIM attribute, subattribute,
   175	and schema uri to a table field. You will need to customize this to your
   176	particular database schema.
   177	
   178	The ``SQLQuery.sql`` method returns SQL that can be used as the first
   179	argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()`` with your favorite DB engine.
   180	If you are using a database that requires a replacement character other than '%s',
   181	then you can subclass the ``SQLQuery`` class and override the ``placeholder`` class
   182	level variable. See the query module and unit tests for an example of this subclassing
   183	with SQLite.
   184	
   185	The ``SQLQuery.params`` method returns a list of items that can be used as the
   186	second argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()``.
   187	
   188	Django
   189	------
   190	
   191	If you would like to produce a `Django Q`_ object instead of a raw SQL query, you can pass
   192	a SCIM filter query and attribute map to the ``get_query`` function from the module
   193	``scim2_filter_parser.transpilers.django_q_object``. For example::
   194	
   195	    get_query(scim_query: str, attr_map: Mapping)
   196	
   197	This Q object can then be passed to a Django filter query like so::
   198	
   199	    query = get_query(scim_query, attr_map)
   200	    User.objects.filter(query)

$$f(x)=\begin{cases}1 & \text{if } |x|=0 \\ x_1+1 & \text{if } |x|=1 \\

$$f(\{n,n\})=n+1$$

To evaluate the limit \(\lim_{x \to \infty} \sqrt{x} \left( \sqrt[3]{x+1} - \sqrt[3]{x-1} \right)\), we can use several approaches including binomial expansion, the mean value theorem, and algebraic manipulation.

1. **Binomial Expansion**:
   - Approximate \((x + 1)^{1/3}\) and \((x - 1)^{1/3}\) using binomial expansion for large \(x\):
     \[
     (x + 1)^{1/3} \approx x^{1/3} \left(1 + \frac{1}{3x} - \frac{1}{9x^2} + \cdots \right)
     \]
     \[
     (x - 1)^{1/3} \approx x^{1/3} \left(1 - \frac{1}{3x} - \frac{1}{9x^2} + \cdots \right)
     \]
   - Subtract the expansions:
     \[
     (x + 1)^{1/3} - (x - 1)^{1/3} \approx x^{1/3} \left( \frac{2}{3x} - \frac{2}{9x^2} + \cdots \right) = \frac{2}{3} x^{-2/3} - \frac{2}{9} x^{-5/3} + \cdots
     \]
   - Multiply by \(\sqrt{x}\):
     \[
     \sqrt{x} \left( \frac{2}{3} x^{-2/3} - \frac{2}{9} x^{-5/3} + \cdots \right) = \frac{2}{3} x^{-1/6} - \frac{2}{9} x^{-7/6} + \cdots
     \]
   - As \(x \to \infty\), the terms \(x^{-1/6}\) and \(x^{-7/6}\) approach zero.

2. **Mean Value Theorem**:
   - Let \(f(t) = t^{1/3}\). By the mean value theorem, there exists \(c \in (x - 1, x + 1)\) such that:
     \[
     f(x + 1) - f(x - 1) = f'(c) \cdot 2
     \]
   - \(f'(c) = \frac{1}{3} c^{-2/3}\). For large \(x\), \(c \approx x\), so:
     \[
     f'(c) \approx \frac{1}{3} x^{-2/3}
     \]
   - Multiply by \(\sqrt{x}\):
     \[
     \sqrt{x} \cdot \frac{2}{3} x^{-2/3} = \frac{2}{3} x^{-1/6} \to 0 \text{ as } x \to \infty
     \]

3. **Algebraic Manipulation**:
   - Use the identity \(a - b = \frac{a^3 - b^3}{a^2 + ab + b^2}\) with \(a = (x + 1)^{1/3}\) and \(b = (x - 1)^{1/3}\):
     \[
     a - b = \frac{2}{(x + 1)^{2/3} + (x + 1)^{1/3}(x - 1)^{1/3} + (x - 1)^{2/3}}
     \]
   - For large \(x\), the denominator approximates to \(3x^{2/3}\):
     \[
     \sqrt{x} \cdot \frac{2}{3x^{2/3}} = \frac{2}{3} x^{-1/6} \to 0 \text{ as } x \to \infty
     \]

All approaches confirm that the limit is zero. Thus, the final answer is:

Okay, so I need to find the auxiliary equation for the given ordinary differential equation (ODE) with constant coefficients: \((x^2D^2 + xD + 1)y = \sin(2\log x)\sin(\log x)\). Hmm, let me start by recalling what an auxiliary equation is. From my classes, I remember that for linear ODEs with constant coefficients, we often use characteristic equations, which are also called auxiliary equations. These are polynomial equations obtained by replacing the differential operator \(D\) with a variable, usually \(m\), and solving for \(m\). The roots of this equation help determine the general solution to the homogeneous equation.

But wait, the given differential equation here is \((x^2D^2 + xD + 1)y = \sin(2\log x)\sin(\log x)\). The operator here is \(x^2D^2 + xD + 1\), which involves variable coefficients since \(x\) is multiplied with the differential operators. However, the problem states that it's an ODE with constant coefficients. That seems contradictory. Maybe there's a substitution that can convert this into an equation with constant coefficients?

I think when the differential equation has terms like \(x^2D^2\), \(xD\), etc., it might be a Cauchy-Euler equation. Cauchy-Euler equations are linear ODEs with variable coefficients, but they can be transformed into constant coefficient equations by using a substitution. The standard form of a Cauchy-Euler equation is \(ax^2y'' + bxy' + cy = f(x)\), which looks similar to our given equation. So, if this is a Cauchy-Euler equation, then we can make the substitution \(t = \log x\) to convert it into a constant coefficient ODE.

Let me try that. Let \(t = \log x\). Then, \(x = e^t\), and we can express the derivatives with respect to \(x\) in terms of derivatives with respect to \(t\). 

First, compute \(dy/dx\). Using the chain rule: \(\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{dt} \cdot \frac{dt}{dx} = \frac{dy}{dt} \cdot \frac{1}{x}\). So, \(x\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{dt}\).

Next, compute the second derivative. Let's denote \(D = \frac{d}{dx}\), so \(D = \frac{d}{dx} = \frac{d}{dt} \cdot \frac{dt}{dx} = \frac{1}{x}\frac{d}{dt}\). Therefore, \(D^2 = \frac{d^2}{dx^2} = \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{x}\frac{d}{dt}\right)\). Let's compute that:

First derivative operator: \(D = \frac{1}{x}\frac{d}{dt}\).

Second derivative: \(D^2 = \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{x}\frac{d}{dt}\right) = -\frac{1}{x^2}\frac{d}{dt} + \frac{1}{x}\frac{d}{dx}\frac{d}{dt}\).

Now, \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{d}{dt} = \frac{d}{dt}\frac{d}{dx}\) since mixed partial derivatives commute (assuming the function is nice). Wait, actually, no. Let's see. \(\frac{d}{dt} = x\frac{d}{dx}\), so \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{d}{dt} = \frac{d}{dx}(x\frac{d}{dx}) = \frac{d}{dx} + x\frac{d^2}{dx^2}\). Hmm, maybe there's a better way to compute \(D^2\).

Alternatively, using the chain rule again. Let's denote \(D_t = \frac{d}{dt}\). Then, since \(t = \log x\), \(dt/dx = 1/x\), so \(D = \frac{d}{dx} = \frac{1}{x} D_t\). Therefore, \(x D = D_t\), and \(x^2 D^2 = x D (x D) - x D\). Wait, this might be a better approach.

Let me recall that for the Cauchy-Euler equation, when we make the substitution \(t = \log x\), the operator \(x D\) becomes \(D_t\), and \(x^2 D^2\) becomes \(D_t(D_t - 1)\). Let me verify this.

Starting with \(x D = D_t\), as we saw earlier. Then, \(x^2 D^2 = x D (x D) - x D\). Applying \(x D\) to \(x D\), we have \(x D (x D y) = x D (x y') = x (y' + x y'')\). Which is \(x y' + x^2 y''\). Therefore, \(x^2 D^2 = x D (x D) - x D = (x D)^2 - x D\). Since \(x D = D_t\), this becomes \(D_t^2 - D_t\). Therefore, \(x^2 D^2 = D_t(D_t - 1)\).

So, substituting into the original operator \(x^2 D^2 + x D + 1\), we get:

\(D_t(D_t - 1) + D_t + 1 = D_t^2 - D_t + D_t + 1 = D_t^2 + 1\).

Therefore, the original differential equation \(x^2 D^2 y + x D y + y = \sin(2 \log x) \sin(\log x)\) transforms into \(D_t^2 y + y = \sin(2 t) \sin(t)\), where \(t = \log x\).

So, now we have a constant coefficient ODE in terms of \(t\):

\(y''(t) + y(t) = \sin(2t) \sin(t)\).

Therefore, the auxiliary equation is obtained by considering the homogeneous equation \(y'' + y = 0\). The characteristic (auxiliary) equation is \(m^2 + 1 = 0\). Solving this gives \(m = \pm i\), which are complex roots. But since the problem only asks for the auxiliary equation, not its roots or the solution, the answer is \(m^2 + 1 = 0\).

Wait, but let me make sure I didn't skip any steps. The original equation was converted to a constant coefficient equation via substitution, and then the auxiliary equation is for the transformed equation. The problem statement mentions "the ordinary differential equation with constant coefficients", which probably refers to the transformed equation after the substitution. Therefore, the auxiliary equation is indeed \(m^2 + 1 = 0\).

But just to be thorough, let's check again. The original equation is a Cauchy-Euler equation, which typically requires the substitution \(t = \log x\) to turn it into a constant coefficient linear ODE. Once we do that substitution, the equation becomes \(y''(t) + y(t) = \sin(2t)\sin(t)\), as I found. Then, the homogeneous part is \(y'' + y = 0\), whose auxiliary equation is \(m^2 + 1 = 0\). That seems correct.

Alternatively, if the question had asked for the auxiliary equation associated directly with the original equation (even though it's variable coefficient), but the problem says "with constant coefficients", which suggests that perhaps we need to consider the equation after substitution. Because the original equation isn't constant coefficient, but after substitution, it is. So, the auxiliary equation is for the transformed constant coefficient equation.

Therefore, the answer is \(m^2 + 1 = 0\). So, boxed, it's \boxed{m^2 + 1 = 0}.

To find the auxiliary equation for the given ordinary differential equation \((x^2D^2 + xD + 1)y = \sin(2\log x)\sin(\log x)\), we recognize it as a Cauchy-Euler equation. We use the substitution \(t = \log x\) to convert it into a constant coefficient ODE.

1. **Substitution**: Let \(t = \log x\). Then, \(x = e^t\) and the differential operators transform as follows:
   - \(x \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{dy}{dt}\)
   - \(x^2 \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{d^2y}{dt^2} - \frac{dy}{dt}\)

2. **Transforming the Equation**: Substituting these into the original equation, we get:
   \[
   x^2 D^2 y + x D y + y = \sin(2 \log x) \sin(\log x)
   \]
   transforms into:
   \[
   \left(\frac{d^2y}{dt^2} - \frac{dy}{dt}\right) + \frac{dy}{dt} + y = \sin(2t) \sin(t)
   \]
   Simplifying, we obtain:
   \[
   \frac{d^2y}{dt^2} + y = \sin(2t) \sin(t)
   \]

3. **Auxiliary Equation**: The homogeneous part of the transformed equation is \(\frac{d^2y}{dt^2} + y = 0\). The auxiliary equation for this is:
   \[
   m^2 + 1 = 0
   \]

tool: Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /testbed/src/scim2_filter_parser/parser.py:
     1	'''
     2	This file defines the parser class that is used to parse a given SCIM query.
     3	
     4	See https://tools.ietf.org/html/rfc7644#section-3.4.2.2 for more details
     5	
     6	::
     7	
     8	    SCIM filters MUST conform to the following ABNF [RFC5234] rules as
     9	    specified below:
    10	
    11	      FILTER    = attrExp / logExp / valuePath / *1"not" "(" FILTER ")"
    12	
    13	      valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
    14	                  ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
    15	
    16	      valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
    17	
    18	      attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
    19	                  (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
    20	
    21	      logExp    = FILTER SP ("and" / "or") SP FILTER
    22	
    23	      compValue = false / null / true / number / string
    24	                  ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
    25	
    26	      compareOp = "eq" / "ne" / "co" /
    27	                         "sw" / "ew" /
    28	                         "gt" / "lt" /
    29	                         "ge" / "le"
    30	
    31	      attrPath  = [URI ":"] ATTRNAME *1subAttr
    32	                  ; SCIM attribute name
    33	                  ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
    34	
    35	      ATTRNAME  = ALPHA *(nameChar)
    36	
    37	      nameChar  = "-" / "_" / DIGIT / ALPHA
    38	
    39	      subAttr   = "." ATTRNAME
    40	                  ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
    41	
    42	                Figure 1: ABNF Specification of SCIM Filters
    43	
    44	    In the above ABNF rules, the "compValue" (comparison value) rule is
    45	    built on JSON Data Interchange format ABNF rules as specified in
    46	    [RFC7159], "DIGIT" and "ALPHA" are defined per Appendix B.1 of
    47	    [RFC5234], and "URI" is defined per Appendix A of [RFC3986].
    48	
    49	    Filters MUST be evaluated using the following order of operations, in
    50	    order of precedence:
    51	
    52	    1.  Grouping operators
    53	
    54	    2.  Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and",
    55	        which takes precedence over "or"
    56	
    57	    3.  Attribute operators
    58	
    59	    If the specified attribute in a filter expression is a multi-valued
    60	    attribute, the filter matches if any of the values of the specified
    61	    attribute match the specified criterion; e.g., if a User has multiple
    62	    "emails" values, only one has to match for the entire User to match.
    63	    For complex attributes, a fully qualified sub-attribute MUST be
    64	    specified using standard attribute notation (Section 3.10).  For
    65	    example, to filter by userName, the parameter value is "userName".
    66	    To filter by first name, the parameter value is "name.givenName".
    67	
    68	    When applying a comparison (e.g., "eq") or presence filter (e.g.,
    69	    "pr") to a defaulted attribute, the service provider SHALL use the
    70	    value that was returned to the client that last created or modified
    71	    the attribute.
    72	
    73	    Providers MAY support additional filter operations if they choose.
    74	    Providers MUST decline to filter results if the specified filter
    75	    operation is not recognized and return an HTTP 400 error with a
    76	    "scimType" error of "invalidFilter" and an appropriate human-readable
    77	    response as per Section 3.12.  For example, if a client specified an
    78	    unsupported operator named 'regex', the service provider should
    79	    specify an error response description identifying the client error,
    80	    e.g., 'The operator 'regex' is not supported.'
    81	
    82	    When comparing attributes of type String, the case sensitivity for
    83	    String type attributes SHALL be determined by the attribute's
    84	    "caseExact" characteristic (see Section 2.2 of [RFC7643]).
    85	
    86	    Clients MAY query by schema or schema extensions by using a filter
    87	    expression including the "schemas" attribute (as shown in Figure 2).
    88	'''

Okay, so I need to find the limit as x approaches 0 of (1/tan²x - 1/x²). Hmm, let me start by understanding what's going on here. When x approaches 0, tanx behaves like x, right? So tanx ≈ x + x³/3 + ... So tan²x ≈ x² + 2x⁴/3 + ... Therefore, 1/tan²x is approximately 1/x² - 2/3 + ... So if I subtract 1/x² from that, the 1/x² terms cancel out, and I'm left with -2/3. So maybe the limit is -2/3? But wait, let me check that properly step by step because maybe my approximation is too rough.

Alternatively, I can write the expression as (1/tan²x - 1/x²). Let's combine these two terms into a single fraction. To do that, I need a common denominator, which would be x² tan²x. So:

[ x² - tan²x ] / [x² tan²x]

So the expression becomes (x² - tan²x)/(x² tan²x). Now, as x approaches 0, both numerator and denominator approach 0. So we can try applying L’Hospital’s Rule. But since both numerator and denominator approach 0, we can apply L’Hospital once, but that might get complicated. Alternatively, expand tanx in terms of Taylor series around 0.

Recall that tanx = x + x³/3 + 2x⁵/15 + ... So tan²x = (x + x³/3 + ...)² = x² + 2x⁴/3 + (2x⁶)/15 + ... So tan²x ≈ x² + (2/3)x⁴. Therefore, the numerator x² - tan²x ≈ x² - (x² + (2/3)x⁴) = - (2/3)x⁴.

The denominator x² tan²x ≈ x²*(x² + (2/3)x⁴) ≈ x⁴ + (2/3)x⁶ ≈ x⁴.

Therefore, the expression becomes (-2/3 x⁴)/x⁴ = -2/3. Therefore, the limit is -2/3. That seems to confirm my initial thought.

   141	    #                               ; 0 or 1 "not"s

Therefore, the entire expression becomes ( -2x⁴/3 + ... ) / (x⁴ - x⁶/3 ) ≈ (-2x⁴/3)/x⁴ = -2/3 as x approaches 0. So that confirms again the limit is -2/3.

Alternatively, another approach: use L’Hospital’s Rule. Let's see. Let me try that. Let's write the original expression as:

lim_{x→0} [1/tan²x - 1/x²] = lim_{x→0} [cot²x - 1/x²]. Let me write it as [cotx - 1/x][cotx + 1/x]. But not sure if that helps. Alternatively, considering the expression as [x² - tan²x]/[x² tan²x]. Let me call this f(x)/g(x), where f(x) = x² - tan²x, g(x) = x² tan²x.

As x→0, f(x)→0 and g(x)→0. So we can apply L’Hospital’s Rule, which says lim f/g = lim f’/g’ provided that limit exists. Let's compute derivatives.

First, f(x) = x² - tan²x. Then f’(x) = 2x - 2 tanx sec²x.

g(x) = x² tan²x. Then g’(x) = 2x tan²x + x² * 2 tanx sec²x = 2x tan²x + 2x² tanx sec²x.

So now, the limit becomes lim_{x→0} [2x - 2 tanx sec²x] / [2x tan²x + 2x² tanx sec²x].

Hmm, still looks complicated. Let's evaluate numerator and denominator as x→0.

First, numerator: 2x - 2 tanx sec²x. Let's approximate each term. tanx ~ x + x³/3, sec²x = 1 + tan²x ~ 1 + x². Therefore, tanx sec²x ~ (x + x³/3)(1 + x²) ≈ x + x³ + x³/3 ≈ x + (4/3)x³. Multiply by 2: 2x + (8/3)x³. So the numerator 2x - 2 tanx sec²x ≈ 2x - [2x + (8/3)x³] = - (8/3)x³.

Denominator: 2x tan²x + 2x² tanx sec²x. Let's approximate each term. tan²x ~ x² + (2/3)x⁴, so 2x tan²x ~ 2x*(x²) = 2x³. Then tanx ~x, sec²x ~1 + x², so 2x² tanx sec²x ~2x²*x*(1 + x²) ≈2x³. Therefore, denominator ≈2x³ + 2x³ =4x³.

So the expression becomes (-8/3 x³)/4x³ = (-8/3)/4 = -2/3. So again, the same result. So even using L’Hospital once, with approximations, we get the result. But perhaps to be rigorous, we might need to apply L’Hospital multiple times? Let me check.

Wait, after the first derivative, we have:

Numerator: 2x - 2 tanx sec²x

Denominator: 2x tan²x + 2x² tanx sec²x

If I plug x=0 directly into numerator and denominator, we get 0 - 0 = 0 in numerator and 0 + 0 = 0 in denominator. So it's still 0/0. So we need to apply L’Hospital again. Let's compute the second derivative.

First, find f’’(x) and g’’(x):

f’(x) = 2x - 2 tanx sec²x

f’’(x) = 2 - 2 [ d/dx (tanx sec²x) ]

Let me compute derivative of tanx sec²x. Let’s denote u = tanx, v = sec²x. Then d/dx (uv) = u’v + uv’.

We know that u’ = sec²x, v’ = 2 sec²x tanx.

Therefore, derivative is sec²x * sec²x + tanx * 2 sec²x tanx = sec⁴x + 2 tan²x sec²x.

Therefore, f’’(x) = 2 - 2[sec⁴x + 2 tan²x sec²x] = 2 - 2 sec⁴x - 4 tan²x sec²x.

Now, g’(x) = 2x tan²x + 2x² tanx sec²x

Compute g’’(x):

First term: d/dx [2x tan²x] = 2 tan²x + 2x * 2 tanx sec²x = 2 tan²x + 4x tanx sec²x

Second term: d/dx [2x² tanx sec²x] = 4x tanx sec²x + 2x² [ d/dx (tanx sec²x) ]

We already computed d/dx (tanx sec²x) above as sec⁴x + 2 tan²x sec²x.

Therefore, second term derivative: 4x tanx sec²x + 2x² [ sec⁴x + 2 tan²x sec²x ]

So overall, g’’(x) = 2 tan²x + 4x tanx sec²x + 4x tanx sec²x + 2x² sec⁴x + 4x² tan²x sec²x

Combine like terms:

= 2 tan²x + 8x tanx sec²x + 2x² sec⁴x + 4x² tan²x sec²x

Now, evaluating f’’(x) and g’’(x) as x→0:

First, compute f’’(x):

= 2 - 2 sec⁴x - 4 tan²x sec²x

secx = 1/cosx ~1 + x²/2 as x→0. So sec⁴x ~ (1 + x²/2)^4 ≈1 + 2x² + ... Similarly, tan²x ~x², sec²x ~1 + x². So tan²x sec²x ~x²(1 + x²) ~x². Therefore, f’’(x) ≈2 - 2(1 + 2x²) -4x²(1 + x²) ≈2 - 2 -4x² -4x² ≈-8x². So f’’(x) ~-8x².

g’’(x):

= 2 tan²x + 8x tanx sec²x + 2x² sec⁴x + 4x² tan²x sec²x

tan²x ~x², tanx ~x, sec²x ~1, sec⁴x ~1. So:

≈2x² + 8x*x*1 + 2x²*1 + 4x²*x²*1 ≈2x² +8x² +2x² +4x⁴≈12x² +4x⁴≈12x².

Therefore, f’’(x)/g’’(x) ≈(-8x²)/12x²≈-8/12 = -2/3. Therefore, lim_{x→0} f’’(x)/g’’(x)= -2/3. Therefore, after applying L’Hospital twice, we get the limit as -2/3.

Therefore, the original limit is indeed -2/3.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{-\dfrac{2}{3}}
</think>

To evaluate the limit \(\lim_{x \to 0} \left(\dfrac{1}{\tan^2 x} - \dfrac{1}{x^2} \right)\), we can use several methods. Here, we use the Taylor series expansion approach and trigonometric identities.

First, we rewrite the expression as a single fraction:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{1}{\tan^2 x} - \frac{1}{x^2} \right) = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x^2 - \tan^2 x}{x^2 \tan^2 x}
\]

Next, we expand \(\tan x\) and \(\sin x\) using their Taylor series around 0:
\[
\tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \cdots \implies \tan^2 x = x^2 + \frac{2x^4}{3} + \cdots
\]
\[
\sin x = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \frac{x^5}{120} - \cdots \implies \sin^2 x = x^2 - \frac{x^4}{3} + \cdots
\]

Using these expansions, we approximate the numerator and denominator:
\[
x^2 - \tan^2 x \approx x^2 - \left( x^2 + \frac{2x^4}{3} \right) = -\frac{2x^4}{3}
\]
\[
x^2 \tan^2 x \approx x^2 \left( x^2 + \frac{2x^4}{3} \right) \approx x^4
\]

Thus, the expression simplifies to:
\[
\frac{-\frac{2x^4}{3}}{x^4} = -\frac{2}{3}
\]

We can also verify this using trigonometric identities and known limits. We know:
\[
\lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{1}{\sin^2 x} - \frac{1}{x^2} \right) = \frac{1}{3}
\]

Rewriting the original expression:
\[
\frac{\cos^2 x}{\sin^2 x} - \frac{1}{x^2} = \frac{\cos^2 x - 1}{\sin^2 x} + \left( \frac{1}{\sin^2 x} - \frac{1}{x^2} \right)
\]
\[
= -\frac{\sin^2 x}{\sin^2 x} + \left( \frac{1}{\sin^2 x} - \frac{1}{x^2} \right) = -1 + \frac{1}{3} = -\frac{2}{3}
\]

Thus, the limit is:
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{2}{3}}
\]<#sep#>， where l 是 length:

<#sep#>Determine the minimum sample size required such that the probability of at least two people being a match at all six genetic markers exceeds 0.001, given that the probability of a match at each individual marker is 1/9.

Okay, so I need to figure out the minimum sample size (number of people) required such that the probability that at least two people share all six genetic markers is more than 0.001. Each genetic marker has a 1/9 chance of matching. Hmm, this seems similar to the birthday problem but with more specific probabilities.

First, let me recall the classic birthday problem. In that problem, we calculate the probability that at least two people share a birthday, and it turns out you only need about 23 people for a 50% chance. But here, instead of 365 days, we have six independent genetic markers, each with a probability of 1/9 of matching. Wait, but the question is about two people matching at all six markers. So, it's not just matching at least one marker, but all six. That's a key difference.

Let me break it down. For two people to be a match at all six genetic markers, each of the six markers must independently match. Since each marker has a 1/9 chance, the probability that two people match at all six markers would be (1/9)^6. Let me compute that. (1/9)^6 is 1 divided by 9^6. 9^2 is 81, 9^3 is 729, 9^6 is (9^3)^2 = 729^2. Let me calculate 729*729. 700*700=490,000, 700*29=20,300, 29*700=20,300, 29*29=841. So adding those together: (700+29)^2 = 729^2 = 490,000 + 2*20,300 + 841 = 490,000 + 40,600 + 841 = 531,441. So (1/9)^6 = 1/531,441. So approximately 0.000001881. That's a very small probability for two people to match all six markers.

But we want the probability that at least two people in a sample size of n people have a match at all six markers to exceed 0.001. So, similar to the birthday problem, but with a much lower probability for a single pair. The formula for the probability of at least one match in n people is approximately 1 - e^(-n(n-1)/(2p)), where p is the number of possibilities. Wait, but here p is 531,441, since that's the number of possible combinations for the six markers? Wait, not exactly. Wait, in the birthday problem, p is the number of days, so the probability that two people share a birthday is 1/p. Here, the probability that two people match on all six markers is (1/9)^6 = 1/531,441, so that's like p = 531,441. So, the formula would be similar.

Alternatively, the exact probability is 1 minus the probability that all pairs do not match. So, for n people, the number of pairs is C(n,2) = n(n-1)/2. The probability that a single pair does not match is 1 - 1/531,441. Therefore, the probability that no two people match is (1 - 1/531,441)^(C(n,2)). So, the probability that at least two people match is 1 - (1 - 1/531,441)^(n(n-1)/2). We want this probability to be greater than 0.001, so:

1 - (1 - 1/531,441)^(n(n-1)/2) > 0.001

Which implies:

(1 - 1/531,441)^(n(n-1)/2) < 0.999

Taking natural logs on both sides:

ln[(1 - 1/531,441)^(n(n-1)/2)] < ln(0.999)

Using the power rule:

(n(n-1)/2) * ln(1 - 1/531,441) < ln(0.999)

Divide both sides by ln(1 - 1/531,441), which is negative, so inequality flips:

n(n-1)/2 > ln(0.999) / ln(1 - 1/531,441)

Compute the right-hand side. Let's calculate ln(0.999). Approximately, since ln(1 - x) ≈ -x - x^2/2 - x^3/3 - ... for small x. So ln(0.999) ≈ -0.001 - 0.0000005 ≈ -0.0010005.

Similarly, ln(1 - 1/531,441) ≈ -1/531,441 - (1/531,441)^2 / 2. Since 1/531,441 is about 1.881e-6, so squared is about 3.54e-12, so negligible. So approximately, ln(1 - 1/531,441) ≈ -1.881e-6.

So the right-hand side is approximately (-0.0010005) / (-1.881e-6) ≈ (0.0010005) / (1.881e-6) ≈ 0.0010005 / 0.000001881 ≈ 532. So approximately 532.

Therefore, n(n-1)/2 > 532

Multiply both sides by 2:

n(n-1) > 1064

So we need to solve for n in n(n-1) > 1064. Let's approximate.

n^2 - n - 1064 > 0

Solving n^2 - n - 1064 = 0

Using quadratic formula:

n = [1 ± sqrt(1 + 4*1064)] / 2

sqrt(1 + 4256) = sqrt(4257). Let's estimate sqrt(4257). 65^2 = 4225, 66^2 = 4356. So sqrt(4257) ≈ 65 + (4257 - 4225)/(4356 - 4225) * 1 ≈ 65 + 32/131 ≈ 65.244.

So n ≈ [1 + 65.244]/2 ≈ 66.244 / 2 ≈ 33.122

Since n must be integer, and n(n-1) must be greater than 1064, so testing n=33: 33*32=1056 <1064. n=34: 34*33=1122>1064. So n=34.

Therefore, the approximation gives n≈34. So the minimum sample size is 34. But wait, this is an approximation. Let's check if the actual value using the exact formula.

First, let's compute ln(0.999) and ln(1 - 1/531441) more accurately.

ln(0.999) is exactly ln(0.999). Let's compute that using calculator.

ln(0.999) ≈ -0.00100050033

ln(1 - 1/531441) = ln(531440/531441) = ln(1 - 1/531441). Let's compute this more accurately. Using the Taylor series:

ln(1 - x) ≈ -x - x^2/2 - x^3/3 - x^4/4 - ...

x = 1/531441 ≈ 0.000001881594

So ln(1 - x) ≈ -0.000001881594 - (0.000001881594)^2 / 2 - (0.000001881594)^3 / 3 - ...

The first term is -1.881594e-6

The second term is - (3.5403e-12)/2 ≈ -1.77015e-12

Third term: - (6.663e-18)/3 ≈ -2.221e-18

So these are negligible. So ln(1 - x) ≈ -1.881594e-6
