```javascript
f=a=>(l=a.length)?(l==1?a[0]+1:Math.log(a.reduce((s,c)=>s+l**c,0))/Math.log(l)):1
```

### Explanation
This function takes an array (list) of real numbers and computes the value based on the given cases:

1. **Empty List**: If the array length is 0, return 1.
2. **Single Element**: If the length is 1, return the first element plus 1.
3. **Logarithmic Calculation**: For arrays longer than 1:
   - Compute the length of the array, `l`.
   - Calculate the sum of `l` raised to each element in the array.
   - Compute the logarithm of this sum with base `l`, using the natural logarithm formula: `ln(sum) / ln(l)`.

The JavaScript implementation efficiently handles all these cases using concise code with nested conditional expressions and the `reduce` function for summing<#sep#>system: You are OpenHands agent, a helpful AI assistant that can interact with a computer to solve tasks.

Compute (1 - 1/531,441)^561 ≈ e^(-561/531,441) ≈ e^(-0.001056). Since for small x, (1 - x)^k ≈ e^{-kx}.

So e^{-0.001056} ≈ 1 - 0.001056 + 0.000000557 ≈ 0.998944. Therefore, the probability of at least one match is ≈1 - 0.998944 = 0.001056, which is just over 0.001. Therefore, n=34 gives probability ≈0.001056>0.001. So n=34 is the minimal sample size.

To determine the minimum sample size required such that the probability of at least two people matching at all six genetic markers exceeds 0.001, we start by calculating the probability of a match at all six markers. The probability of a match at each individual marker is \( \frac{1}{9} \), so the probability of matching at all six markers is \( \left( \frac{1}{9} \right)^6 = \frac{1}{531441} \).

We use the complement rule to find the probability that no two people match at all six markers. For \( n \) people, the number of pairs is \( \binom{n}{2} = \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \). The probability that no two people match is \( \left( 1 - \frac{1}{531441} \right)^{\binom{n}{2}} \). Therefore, the probability that at least two people match is:

\[
1 - \left( 1 - \frac{1}{531441} \right)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}
\]

We need this probability to exceed 0.001:

\[
1 - \left( 1 - \frac{1}{531441} \right)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}} > 0.001
\]

Taking the natural logarithm on both sides:

\[
\left( \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \right) \ln \left( 1 - \frac{1}{531441} \right) < \ln(0.999)
\]

Using the approximation \( \ln(1 - x) \approx -x \) for small \( x \):

\[

assistant: ### 1.1-1.3 Problem Analysis

The issue is with the SCIM filter parser when handling multiple consecutive logical operators:
- **Failing queries**: 3+ consecutive "or" or "and" operators
- **Working queries**: Mixed "or" and "and" operators
- **Error**: `scim2_filter_parser.parser.SCIMParserError: Parsing error at: Token(type='OR', value='or', lineno=1, index=52)`

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Parse query with 3+ consecutive "or" operators: `"displayName co \"username\" or nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""`
2. Parse query with 3+ consecutive "and" operators: `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" and userName co \"username\""`
3. Both should fail with parsing error
4. Parse mixed operator query: `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""` - this should work

### 1.5 Best Practices
- Maintain proper operator precedence and associativity
- Ensure left-to-right evaluation for same operators
- Don't break existing mixed operator functionality
- Test edge cases and nesting scenarios

     1	SCIM 2.0 Filter Parser
     2	======================
     3	
     4	|github| |codecov| |docs|
     5	
     6	.. |codecov| image:: https://codecov.io/gh/15five/scim2-filter-parser/branch/master/graph/badge.svg
     7	  :target: https://codecov.io/gh/15five/scim2-filter-parser
     8	
     9	.. |docs| image:: https://readthedocs.org/projects/scim2-filter-parser/badge/?version=latest
    10	  :target: https://scim2-filter-parser.readthedocs.io/en/latest/?badge=latest
    11	  :alt: Documentation Status
    12	
    13	.. |github| image:: https://github.com/15five/scim2-filter-parser/workflows/CI%2FCD/badge.svg
    14	  :target: https://github.com/15five/scim2-filter-parser/actions?workflow=CI%2FCD
    15	  :alt: CI/CD Status
    16	
    17	Description
    18	-----------
    19	
    20	SCIM 2.0 defines queries that look like this::
    21	
    22	    'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
    23	
    24	These can be hard to work with and covert into SQL to run against a database.
    25	
    26	That's where SCIM 2.0 Filter Parser (SFP) can help.
    27	
    28	SFP is broken up into four modules, each handling a different part of
    29	translating a SCIM call into a SQL query.
    30	
    31	The first step is tokenization or lexical analysis where the filter query
    32	is broken down into many tokens that make it up.
    33	
    34	::
    35	
    36	    sfp-lexer 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
    37	
    38	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='emails', lineno=1, index=0)
    39	    Token(type='LBRACKET', value='[', lineno=1, index=6)
    40	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='type', lineno=1, index=7)
    41	    Token(type='EQ', value='eq', lineno=1, index=12)
    42	    Token(type='COMP_VALUE', value='work', lineno=1, index=15)

\[
1 - \left( 1 - \frac{1}{531441} \right)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}} > 0.001
\]

Taking the natural logarithm on both sides:

\[
\left( \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \right) \ln \left( 1 - \frac{1}{531441} \right) < \ln(0.999)
\]

Using the approximation \( \ln(1 - x) \approx -x \) for small \( x \):

\[
\frac{n(n-1)}{2} \left( -\frac{1}{531441} \right) < \ln(0.999)
\]

Multiplying both sides by -1 (reversing the inequality):

\[
\frac{n(n-1)}{2} > \frac{-\ln(0.999)}{1/531441}
\]

Calculating the right-hand side:

\[
-\ln(0.999) \approx 0.0010005 \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{0.0010005}{1/531441} \approx 531.44
\]

Thus:

\[
\frac{n(n-1)}{2} > 531.44 \implies n(n-1) > 1062.88
\]

Solving the quadratic equation \( n^2 - n - 1064 > 0 \) using the quadratic formula:

\[
n = \frac{1 + \sqrt{1 + 4 \times 1064}}{2} \approx \frac{1 + 65.244}{2} \approx 33.122
\]

Testing integer values, we find \( n = 34 \) gives \( 34 \times 33 = 1122 \), which satisfies the inequality, while \( n = 33 \) gives \( 33 \times 32 = 1056 \), which does not. Therefore, the minimum sample size required is:

\boxed{34}
\]<#sep#>human: The second semester starts at the University of Pavlopolis. After vacation in Vičkopolis Noora needs to return to Pavlopolis and continue her study.

Sometimes (or quite often) there are teachers who do not like you. Incidentally Noora also has one such teacher. His name is Yury Dmitrievich and he teaches graph theory. Yury Dmitrievich doesn't like Noora, so he always gives the girl the most difficult tasks. So it happened this time.

The teacher gives Noora a tree with n vertices. Vertices are numbered with integers from 1 to n. The length of all the edges of this tree is 1. Noora chooses a set of simple paths that pairwise don't intersect in edges. However each vertex should belong to at least one of the selected path.

For each of the selected paths, the following is done:

  1. We choose exactly one edge (u, v) that belongs to the path. 
  2. On the selected edge (u, v) there is a point at some selected distance x from the vertex u and at distance 1 - x from vertex v. But the distance x chosen by Noora arbitrarily, i. e. it can be different for different edges. 
  3. One of the vertices u or v is selected. The point will start moving to the selected vertex. 



Let us explain how the point moves by example. Suppose that the path consists of two edges (v1, v2) and (v2, v3), the point initially stands on the edge (v1, v2) and begins its movement to the vertex v1. Then the point will reach v1, then "turn around", because the end of the path was reached, further it will move in another direction to vertex v2, then to vertex v3, then "turn around" again, then move to v2 and so on. The speed of the points is 1 edge per second. For example, for 0.5 second the point moves to the length of the half of an edge.

A stopwatch is placed at each vertex of the tree. The time that the stopwatches indicate at start time is 0 seconds. Then at the starting moment of time, all points simultaneously start moving from the selected positions to selected directions along the selected paths, and stopwatches are simultaneously started. When one of the points reaches the vertex v, the stopwatch at the vertex v is automatically reset, i.e. it starts counting the time from zero.

Denote by resv the maximal time that the stopwatch at the vertex v will show if the point movement continues infinitely. Noora is asked to select paths and points on them so that res1 is as minimal as possible. If there are several solutions to do this, it is necessary to minimize res2, then res3, res4, ..., resn.

Help Noora complete the teacher's task.

For the better understanding of the statement, see the explanation for the example.
Input

The first line contains single integer n (2 ≤ n ≤ 100) — number of vertices in the given tree.

Each of next n - 1 lines contains two integers u and v (1 ≤ u, v ≤ n, u ≠ v) — vertices connected by an edge.

Guaranteed that input defines a valid tree.

Output

In the first line print single integer paths — number of paths you want to choose.

In the next paths lines print path's descriptions:

  1. Single integer len — number of edges in the current path. 
  2. len integers — indices of the edges in the path. The edges are numbered from 1 to n - 1 in order they are given in input. 
  3. Two integers u and v — means that you put point on the edge between vertices u and v (obviously the edge should belong to the path) and a point will start moving to the vertex v. Note that order of printing of the edge's ends is important. For example if you print "1 2" (without quotes), then point will start moving to vertex 2; but if you print "2 1" (without quotes), then point will start moving to vertex 1. 
  4. Single real number x (0 ≤ x ≤ 1) — distance between point and vertex u (the same vertex that you print first in the third paragraph). 

Scoring

Judge system will generate array res using the output data provided by the participant. Also system will generate array resOptimal by the jury answer. Your answer will be accepted if only for each i (1 ≤ i ≤ n) the following is satisfied: <image>.

Example

Input

3
1 2
2 3


Output

2
1 1 1 2 0.6666666666
1 2 2 3 0.6666666666

In 0.(3) seconds points will be located in following way (before stopwatch reset):

After stopwatch reset:

<image>

In 1.0 second after the start of moving:

<image>

In 1.(3) seconds after the start of moving (after stopwatch reset):

<image>

Finally, in 2 seconds after the start of moving points return to their initial positions.

<image>

This process will continue infinitely.

   173	
   174	The attribute_map (``attr_map``) is a mapping of SCIM attribute, subattribute,
   175	and schema uri to a table field. You will need to customize this to your
   176	particular database schema.
   177	
   178	The ``SQLQuery.sql`` method returns SQL that can be used as the first
   179	argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()`` with your favorite DB engine.
   180	If you are using a database that requires a replacement character other than '%s',
   181	then you can subclass the ``SQLQuery`` class and override the ``placeholder`` class
   182	level variable. See the query module and unit tests for an example of this subclassing
   183	with SQLite.
   184	
   185	The ``SQLQuery.params`` method returns a list of items that can be used as the
   186	second argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()``.
   187	
   188	Django
   189	------
   190	
   191	If you would like to produce a `Django Q`_ object instead of a raw SQL query, you can pass
   192	a SCIM filter query and attribute map to the ``get_query`` function from the module
   193	``scim2_filter_parser.transpilers.django_q_object``. For example::
   194	
   195	    get_query(scim_query: str, attr_map: Mapping)
   196	
   197	This Q object can then be passed to a Django filter query like so::
   198	
   199	    query = get_query(scim_query, attr_map)
   200	    User.objects.filter(query)
   201	
   202	Please note that you will need to install the Django Query extra like for this feature to be available::
   203	
   204	    pip install scim2-filter-parser[django-query]
   205	
   206	.. _`Django Q`: https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/3.1/topics/db/queries/#complex-lookups-with-q-objects
   207	
   208	Speed
   209	-----
   210	
   211	SFP is pretty fast. Check out the speed_test.py script for details on the long and short
   212	filter queries tested. SFP transpiled a short filter query into SQL in under 54 microseconds.
   213	For a longer query, SFP only took 273 microseconds.
   214

   216

As n becomes large, the term (sqrt(2πn))^{1/n} approaches 1 because the exponent 1/n approaches 0. Therefore, (n!)^{1/n} ≈ n/e, which clearly tends to infinity as n approaches infinity.

The key point is that Stirling's approximation gives us a way to approximate the factorial, and when taking the x-th root, the dominant term is x/e, which goes to infinity. Therefore, the limit is infinity. 

Alternatively, perhaps using the concept that the geometric mean of the first n integers is (n!)^{1/n}. The geometric mean of 1, 2, ..., n is (n!)^{1/n}. We know that the arithmetic mean of these numbers is (n+1)/2, which tends to infinity as n approaches infinity. The geometric mean is always less than or equal to the arithmetic mean, but in this case, even though it's less, it still might tend to infinity. For example, the geometric mean of 1, 2, ..., n is approximately n/e, as per Stirling, which does go to infinity. 

Therefore, all these methods lead me to conclude that the limit is infinity. So, the final answer should be infinity.

</think>

To find the limit \(\lim_{x \to \infty} (x!)^{1/x}\), we use Stirling's approximation, which states that \(x! \approx \sqrt{2\pi x} \left(\frac{x}{e}\right)^x\) for large \(x\).

Applying Stirling's approximation, we have:
\[
(x!)^{1/x} \approx \left(\sqrt{2\pi x} \left(\frac{x}{e}\right)^x\right)^{1/x}
\]

Breaking this down, we get:
\[
\left(\sqrt{2\pi x}\right)^{1/x} \left(\left(\frac{x}{e}\right)^x\right)^{1/x}
\]

Simplifying each term separately:
1. \(\left(\sqrt{2\pi x}\right)^{1/x} = (2\pi x)^{1/(2x)}\)
2. \(\left(\left(\frac{x}{e}\right)^x\right)^{1/x} = \frac{x}{e}\)

Next, we analyze the limit of each term as \(x \to \infty\):
1. For the term \((2\pi x)^{1/(2x)}\), we take the natural logarithm:
   \[
   \ln\left((2\pi x)^{1/(2x)}\right) = \frac{\ln(2\pi x)}{2x}
   \]
   As \(x \to \infty\), \(\frac{\ln(2\pi x)}{2x} \to 0\), so \((2\pi x)^{1/(2x)} \to e^0 = 1\).

2. The term \(\frac{x}{e}\) clearly tends to infinity as \(x \to \infty\).

Combining these results, we have:
\[
(x!)^{1/x} \approx 1 \cdot \frac{x}{e} \to \infty \text{ as } x \to \infty
\]

To confirm, we also consider the logarithm of the limit:
\[
\ln\left(\lim_{x \to \infty} (x!)^{1/x}\right) = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1}{x} \ln(x!)
\]
Using Stirling's approximation for \(\ln(x!)\):
\[
\ln(x!) \approx x \ln x - x + \frac{1}{2} \ln(2\pi x)
\]
Thus,
\[
\frac{1}{x} \ln(x!) \approx \ln x - 1 + \frac{\ln(2\pi x)}{2x}
\]
As \(x \to \infty\), \(\frac{\ln(2\pi x)}{2x} \to 0\), leaving \(\ln x - 1 \to \infty\). Therefore, the logarithm of the limit tends to infinity, implying the limit itself is infinity.

Hence, the final answer is:
\[
\boxed{\infty}

________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________________<#sep#>Find the length of the polar curve given by \( r = \sqrt{1 + \cos(2\theta)} \) for \( 0 \leq \theta \leq \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} \).

Okay, so I need to find the length of the polar curve given by \( r = \sqrt{1 + \cos(2\theta)} \) from \( \theta = 0 \) to \( \theta = \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} \). Hmm, polar curves, arc length... I remember there's a formula for the arc length of a polar curve. Let me try to recall.

I think the formula is something like the integral from \( a \) to \( b \) of the square root of \( r^2 + (dr/d\theta)^2 \) dθ. Let me check. Yes, that's right. The general formula for the length \( L \) of a polar curve \( r = r(\theta) \) from \( \theta = a \) to \( \theta = b \) is:

\[
L = \int_{a}^{b} \sqrt{ \left( r \right)^2 + \left( \frac{dr}{d\theta} \right)^2 } \, d\theta
\]

So, I need to compute this integral for the given \( r \). Let me first write down \( r \) again: \( r = \sqrt{1 + \cos(2\theta)} \). Hmm, that square root seems familiar. Wait, there's a trigonometric identity related to \( 1 + \cos(2\theta) \). Let me recall. I think \( 1 + \cos(2\theta) = 2\cos^2(\theta) \). Let me verify that:

Yes, using the double-angle formula: \( \cos(2\theta) = 2\cos^2(\theta) - 1 \), so \( 1 + \cos(2\theta) = 2\cos^2(\theta) \). Perfect! That simplifies the expression under the square root. So, substituting that into \( r \), we have:

\[
r = \sqrt{2\cos^2(\theta)} = \sqrt{2} |\cos(\theta)|
\]

Ah, so \( r \) is \( \sqrt{2} |\cos(\theta)| \). But since we're dealing with \( \theta \) in the interval from 0 to \( \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} \), I need to check if \( \cos(\theta) \) is positive or negative in this interval. Let's see, \( \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} \) is approximately \( \frac{3.1416 \times 1.4142}{4} \approx 1.11 \) radians. Since \( \pi/2 \) is about 1.5708 radians, our interval goes up to about 1.11 radians, which is less than \( \pi/2 \). Therefore, \( \theta \) is in the first quadrant where cosine is positive. Therefore, the absolute value can be removed without changing the sign:

\[
r = \sqrt{2} \cos(\theta)
\]

Okay, so that simplifies the expression for \( r \). Now, let's compute \( dr/d\theta \). Taking the derivative of \( r \):

\[
\frac{dr}{d\theta} = \sqrt{2} (-\sin(\theta)) = -\sqrt{2} \sin(\theta)
\]

So, \( dr/d\theta = -\sqrt{2} \sin(\theta) \). Now, let's plug \( r \) and \( dr/d\theta \) into the arc length formula:

\[
L = \int_{0}^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4}} \sqrt{ \left( \sqrt{2} \cos(\theta) \right)^2 + \left( -\sqrt{2} \sin(\theta) \right)^2 } \, d\theta
\]

Let's compute the terms inside the square root:

First term: \( (\sqrt{2} \cos\theta)^2 = 2 \cos^2\theta \)

Second term: \( (-\sqrt{2} \sin\theta)^2 = 2 \sin^2\theta \)

So adding them together:

\[
2 \cos^2\theta + 2 \sin^2\theta = 2(\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta) = 2(1) = 2
\]

Oh, that's nice! So the integrand simplifies to \( \sqrt{2} \). Therefore, the integral becomes:

\[
L = \int_{0}^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4}} \sqrt{2} \, d\theta = \sqrt{2} \times \left( \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} - 0 \right) = \sqrt{2} \times \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4}
\]

Multiplying \( \sqrt{2} \times \sqrt{2} \) gives 2, so:

\[
L = \frac{2\pi}{4} = \frac{\pi}{2}
\]

Wait, so the length of the curve is \( \pi/2 \)? That seems straightforward. Let me just verify my steps again to make sure I didn't skip anything.

First, recognizing that \( r = \sqrt{1 + \cos2\theta} \) simplifies to \( \sqrt{2} |\cos\theta| \), and since \( \theta \) is between 0 and ~1.11 radians (which is less than \( \pi/2 \)), cosine is positive, so absolute value can be dropped. Then, taking the derivative gives \( -\sqrt{2}\sin\theta \). Squaring both \( r \) and \( dr/d\theta \), adding them gives 2, square root of 2 is the integrand. Then integrating sqrt(2) over the interval gives sqrt(2) multiplied by the upper limit (since lower limit is 0). Then sqrt(2) times pi*sqrt(2)/4 equals (2 pi)/4 = pi/2. So yes, seems correct.

But wait a second, just to double-check, maybe I made a mistake in the trigonometric identity? Let me confirm \( 1 + cos2θ = 2cos²θ. Yes:

cos2θ = 2cos²θ -1, so 1 + cos2θ = 2cos²θ. Correct. So that step is fine.

Then dr/dθ: derivative of sqrt(2)cosθ is -sqrt(2)sinθ. Correct. Squaring that gives 2 sin²θ. Then adding to 2 cos²θ gives 2(sin²θ + cos²θ) = 2*1=2. So sqrt(2). Therefore, integrand is sqrt(2). Therefore, integral is sqrt(2)*(upper limit - lower limit). Upper limit is pi*sqrt(2)/4, lower limit 0. So sqrt(2)*(pi*sqrt(2)/4) = (sqrt(2)*sqrt(2))*pi/4 = 2*pi/4 = pi/2. So yes, that's correct. Therefore, the length is pi/2.

If r = a cosθ, then dr/dθ = -a sinθ. Then,

Integrand sqrt(r^2 + (dr/dθ)^2) = sqrt(a² cos²θ + a² sin²θ) = sqrt(a² (cos²θ + sin²θ)) = a.

Therefore, the integral from -pi/2 to pi/2 is a*(pi/2 - (-pi/2)) = a*pi. So the full circumference is a*pi. Which matches our previous statement. So, for a circle with diameter a, circumference is a*pi.

The key insight was recognizing the trigonometric identity to simplify the equation of the polar curve, which then made the rest of the computation straightforward. It's interesting how these identities can simplify problems so much. Initially, when I saw the square root of 1 + cos2θ, I thought maybe it was a cardioid or something else, but realizing it simplifies to a multiple of |cosθ| was crucial. Then, verifying that in the given interval, cosθ is positive allowed us to drop the absolute value. From there, the derivative was straightforward, and the integrand simplified to a constant, making the integral easy to evaluate. So, all steps seem correct and the answer is pi over two.

**Final Answer**
The length of the polar curve is \boxed{\dfrac{\pi}{2}}.
</think>

To find the length of the polar curve given by \( r = \sqrt{1 + \cos(2\theta)} \) for \( 0 \leq \theta \leq \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} \), we use the arc length formula for polar curves:

\[
L = \int_{a}^{b} \sqrt{ \left( r \right)^2 + \left( \frac{dr}{d\theta} \right)^2 } \, d\theta
\]

First, we simplify the given polar equation using the trigonometric identity \( 1 + \cos(2\theta) = 2\cos^2(\theta) \):

\[
r = \sqrt{1 + \cos(2\theta)} = \sqrt{2\cos^2(\theta)} = \sqrt{2} |\cos(\theta)|
\]

Since \( \theta \) is in the interval \( [0, \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4}] \) where \(\cos(\theta)\) is positive, we can remove the absolute value:

\[
r = \sqrt{2} \cos(\theta)
\]

Next, we compute the derivative \( \frac{dr}{d\theta} \):

\[
\frac{dr}{d\theta} = -\sqrt{2} \sin(\theta)
\]

Substituting \( r \) and \( \frac{dr}{d\theta} \) into the arc length formula:

\[
L = \int_{0}^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4}} \sqrt{ \left( \sqrt{2} \cos(\theta) \right)^2 + \left( -\sqrt{2} \sin(\theta) \right)^2 } \, d\theta
\]

Simplifying the integrand:

\[
\sqrt{2\cos^2(\theta) + 2\sin^2(\theta)} = \sqrt{2(\cos^2(\theta) + \sin^2(\theta))} = \sqrt{2 \cdot 1} = \sqrt{2}
\]

Thus, the integral becomes:

\[
L = \int_{0}^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4}} \sqrt{2} \, d\theta = \sqrt{2} \left[ \theta \right]_{0}^{\frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4}} = \sqrt{2} \left( \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} - 0 \right) = \sqrt{2} \cdot \frac{\pi\sqrt{2}}{4} = \frac{2\pi}{4} = \frac{\pi}{2}
\]

The length of the polar curve is \(\boxed{\dfrac{\pi}{2}}\).<#sep#> time.

   143	    # Since valFilter and Filter have the same structure, we have to

   145	    #
   146	    # attr_path here acts like a namespace for the filter operation inside
   147	    # the brackets. Thus we need to distribute the namespace to the leaf nodes

   168	        return ast.AttrExpr(p[1], p.attr_path, comp_value)
   169

   174	        return ast.LogExpr(p[1], p.filter0, p.filter1)
   175

   177	    #            ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
   178	    @_('FALSE', # noqa F821

   187	    #             ; SCIM attribute name
   188	    #             ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
   189	    @_('ATTRNAME', # noqa F821
   190	       'ATTRNAME sub_attr',
   191	       'SCHEMA_URI ATTRNAME',
   192	       'SCHEMA_URI ATTRNAME sub_attr',
   193	       # Next clause is not in spec but allows us to handle things
   194	       # like 'members[value eq "6784"] eq ""' which are helpful for
   195	       # AttrPath parsing for PATCH calls.

   197	       # Not sure this last clause is in the ABNF spec
   198	       # but Azure/Microsoft uses it so let's be prepared.
   199	       'value_path sub_attr')
   200	    def attr_path(self, p):
   201	        if len(p) == 1:
   202	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   203	
   204	        elif len(p) == 2 and isinstance(p[0], ast.Filter) and isinstance(p[1], ast.SubAttr):
   205	            sub_attr = p[0].namespace.sub_attr
   206	            if sub_attr is not None:
   207	                raise SCIMParserError(f'Parsing error at: {p}')
   208	
   209	            # For easier transpiling, convert complex queries like so:
   210	            # emails[type eq "Primary"].value -> emails.value[type eq "Primary"]
   211	            p[0].namespace.sub_attr = p[1]
   212	
   213	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], None, None)
   214	
   215	        elif len(p) == 2 and isinstance(p[1], ast.SubAttr):
   216	            return ast.AttrPath(p[0], p[1], None)
   217	
   218	        elif len(p) == 2:
   219	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], None, p[0])

   221	        else:
   222	            return ast.AttrPath(p[1], p[2], p[0])

   225	    #             ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
   226	    @_('SUBATTR') # noqa F821
   227	    def sub_attr(self, p):
   228	        return ast.SubAttr(p[0])
   229	
   230	    def error(self, p):
   231	        raise SCIMParserError(f'Parsing error at: {p}')

The cardinality of \( B \) is equal to the cardinality of \( A \), so \(\boxed{|A|}\).

Given that \( A \) is a proper infinite subset of a set \( X \), and \( x \) and \( y \) are two distinct elements of \( X \) not in \( A \), we define \( B = \{x, y\} \cup A \). We need to determine the cardinality of \( B \) in terms of the cardinality of \( A \).

1. **Cardinality of Infinite Sets**: When adding a finite number of elements to an infinite set, the cardinality remains unchanged. This is because the union of an infinite set with a finite set does not increase its cardinality. 
2. **Cardinal Arithmetic**: For any infinite cardinal \( \kappa \) and finite cardinal \( n \), the equation \( \kappa + n = \kappa \) holds. Here, \( |A| \) is an infinite cardinal, and \( \{x, y\} \) is a finite set with cardinality 2.
3. **Conclusion**: The union \( B = A \cup \{x, y\} \) has the same cardinality as \( A \) because the addition of two finite elements to an infinite set does not change its cardinality.

Thus, the cardinality of \( B \) is \(\boxed{|A|}\).<#sep#>'s res array would be [2,1,2]. The first element is res1=2. But could there be a better configuration where res1 is smaller?

     2	This file contains the lexer class that is used to tokenize a given SCIM

    14	   | eq       | equal       | The attribute and operator values must   |

    24	   | sw       | starts with | The entire operator value must be a      |

    30	   |          |             |                                          |
    31	   | ew       | ends with   | The entire operator value must be a      |

The issue is with a SCIM (System for Cross-domain Identity Management) filter parser that fails when processing queries with multiple consecutive "or" or "and" operators. 

**Key technical details:**
- Error: `scim2_filter_parser.parser.SCIMParserError: Parsing error at: Token(type='OR', value='or', lineno=1, index=52)`
- Module: `scim2_filter_parser.parser`

- Mixed queries work correctly

### 1.5 Best Practices

- Maintain backward compatibility with existing working queries

    13	.. |github| image:: https://github.com/15five/scim2-filter-parser/workflows/CI%2FCD/badge.svg

    16	
    17	Description
    18	-----------
    19	
    20	SCIM 2.0 defines queries that look like this::
    21	
    22	    'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
    23	
    24	These can be hard to work with and covert into SQL to run against a database.
    25	
    26	That's where SCIM 2.0 Filter Parser (SFP) can help.
    27	
    28	SFP is broken up into four modules, each handling a different part of
    29	translating a SCIM call into a SQL query.
    30	
    31	The first step is tokenization or lexical analysis where the filter query
    32	is broken down into many tokens that make it up.
    33	
    34	::
    35	
    36	    sfp-lexer 'emails[type eq "work" and value co "@example.com"] or ims[type eq "xmpp" and value co "@foo.com"]'
    37	
    38	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='emails', lineno=1, index=0)

    42	    Token(type='COMP_VALUE', value='work', lineno=1, index=15)
    43	    Token(type='AND', value='and', lineno=1, index=22)
    44	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='value', lineno=1, index=26)
    45	    Token(type='CO', value='co', lineno=1, index=32)
    46	    Token(type='COMP_VALUE', value='@example.com', lineno=1, index=35)

So the ratio is φ⁴.<#sep#>human: Starry is a funny esoteric programming language in which code consists only of +*.,`' where the actual command represented by each of those characters is determined by the number of spaces in front of it. That makes it tricky even to golf fixed-output challenges, because different commands can account for vastly different numbers of bytes. In particular, number literals have a unary representation which makes it necessary to build up larger numbers by operating on smaller ones.
Therefore, this challenge is about writing a program which can golf such Starry programs.
How does Starry work?
(A few details are left unspecified on esolangs, so I'm going with the behaviour of the Ruby interpreter.)
Starry is a stack-based language, with a single stack of arbitrary-precision integer values (which is initially empty).
The only meaningful characters are: 
+*.,`'

and spaces. All other characters are ignored. Each sequence of spaces followed by one of those non-space characters represents a single instruction. The type of instruction depends on the non-space character and the number of spaces. 
The instructions are:
Spaces  Symbol  Meaning
0         +     Invalid opcode.
1         +     Duplicate top of stack.
2         +     Swap top 2 stack elements.
3         +     Rotate top 3 stack elements. That is, send the top stack element
                two positions down. [... 1 2 3] becomes [... 3 1 2].
4         +     Pop and discard top of stack.
n ≥ 5     +     Push n − 5 to stack.
0 mod 5   *     Pop y, pop x, push x + y.
1 mod 5   *     Pop y, pop x, push x − y.
2 mod 5   *     Pop y, pop x, push x * y.
3 mod 5   *     Pop y, pop x, push x / y, rounded towards -∞.
4 mod 5   *     Pop y, pop x, push x % y. The sign of the result matches the sign of y.
0 mod 2   .     Pop a value and print it as a decimal number.
1 mod 2   .     Pop a value and print it as an ASCII character. This throws an error
                if the value is not in the range [0, 255].
n         `     Mark label n.
n         '     Pop a value; if non-zero, jump to label n. 

Note that the interpreter scans the source code for the labels before execution begins, so it's possible to jump forwards as well as backwards.
Of course, Starry also has input commands (using , analogously to .), but those are irrelevant for this challenge.
The Challenge
Given a string, generate a Starry program which takes no input and prints that string exactly to STDOUT.
You may write a program or function, taking input via STDIN (or closest alternative), command-line argument or function argument and outputting the result via STDOUT (or closest alternative), function return value or function (out) parameter.
You may assume that the string is not longer than 128 characters and that it will consist only of printable ASCII characters (code points 0x20 to 0x7E).
Your solution should process any such input in less than 5 minutes on a reasonable desktop machine (there's some leeway to this; if it takes a few minutes more on my laptop I don't mind, but if it takes 15, I'll disqualify it).
Your solution will be tested on a number of different strings listed below. Your score is the total byte count of the corresponding Starry programs. In the case of a tie, the shortest metagolfer wins. That is, don't bother golfing your own code unless there is a tie (which I think will only happen in the case that an optimal solution is possible).
You must not optimise your code towards the specific test cases listed below. Specifically, you shouldn't hardcode hand-crafted solutions for them. Optimising towards classes of strings whose structure is similar to that of the given strings is fine. If I suspect anyone of hardcoding solutions, I reserve the right to replace some or all of the test cases (with strings of comparable structures).

Reference Solution
Here is a really basic reference solution in CJam:
q{S5*\iS*'+S'.}%

You can run it against the entire test suite here. The scores are:

It's the simplest possible approach: push each character's code point as a literal, and then print it. It makes no use of small differences between consecutive characters, integer printing, repetitive parts of the string etc. I'll leave those things to you.
I believe there is a lot of room for improvement. For reference, the shortest handcrafted "Hello, World!" is only 169 bytes long.

In a regular pentagon, the length of a diagonal is φ times the side length. When you form the pentagram, the intersections of the diagonals create segments that are in the golden ratio. Specifically, if a diagonal is divided by an intersection into two parts, the ratio of the whole diagonal to the longer segment is φ, and the ratio of the longer segment to the shorter segment is also φ.    173	
   174	The attribute_map (``attr_map``) is a mapping of SCIM attribute, subattribute,
   175	and schema uri to a table field. You will need to customize this to your
   176	particular database schema.
   177	
   178	The ``SQLQuery.sql`` method returns SQL that can be used as the first
   179	argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()`` with your favorite DB engine.
   180	If you are using a database that requires a replacement character other than '%s',
   181	then you can subclass the ``SQLQuery`` class and override the ``placeholder`` class
   182	level variable. See the query module and unit tests for an example of this subclassing
   183	with SQLite.
   184	
   185	The ``SQLQuery.params`` method returns a list of items that can be used as the
   186	second argument in a call to ``cursor.execute()``.
   187	
   188	Django
   189	------
   190	
   191	If you would like to produce a `Django Q`_ object instead of a raw SQL query, you can pass
   192	a SCIM filter query and attribute map to the ``get_query`` function from the module
   193	``scim2_filter_parser.transpilers.django_q_object``. For example::
   194	
   195	    get_query(scim_query: str, attr_map: Mapping)
   196	
   197	This Q object can then be passed to a Django filter query like so::
   198	
   199	    query = get_query(scim_query, attr_map)
   200	    User.objects.filter(query)
   201	
   202	Please note that you will need to install the Django Query extra like for this feature to be available::
   203	
   204	    pip install scim2-filter-parser[django-query]
   205	
   206	.. _`Django Q`: https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/3.1/topics/db/queries/#complex-lookups-with-q-objects
   207	
   208	Speed
   209	-----
   210	
   211	SFP is pretty fast. Check out the speed_test.py script for details on the long and short
   212	filter queries tested. SFP transpiled a short filter query into SQL in under 54 microseconds.
   213	For a longer query, SFP only took 273 microseconds.
   214	
   215	::
   216	
   217	    ➜  scim2-filter-parser git:(master) ✗ python -m timeit -s "import speed_test" "speed_test.short()"
   218	    10000 loops, best of 3: 53.8 usec per loop
   219	    ➜  scim2-filter-parser git:(master) ✗ python -m timeit -s "import speed_test" "speed_test.long()"
   220	    1000 loops, best of 3: 273 usec per loop

To find the ratio of the area of the original regular pentagon to the smaller pentagon formed inside the pentagram, we start by noting that the golden ratio \(\phi = \frac{1 + \sqrt{5}}{2}\) plays a crucial role in the properties of regular pentagons and pentagrams.

1. **Key Properties of the Regular Pentagon**:
   - The diagonal of a regular pentagon is \(\phi\) times the length of its side.
   - When connecting each vertex of a regular pentagon to every other non-adjacent vertex, the intersections of these diagonals form a smaller regular pentagon inside.

2. **Intersection Points and Scaling Factor**:
   - The intersection of two diagonals in a regular pentagon divides each diagonal into segments with a ratio of \(\phi:1\).
   - The distance from the center of the original pentagon to the vertices of the smaller pentagon is scaled by a factor of \(\frac{1}{\phi^2}\).

3. **Area Ratio Calculation**:
   - The ratio of the areas of similar figures is the square of the ratio of their corresponding linear dimensions.
   - Since the smaller pentagon is inscribed in a circle with radius \(\frac{1}{\phi^2}\) times the radius of the original pentagon (which is 1 for a unit circle), the ratio of their areas is the square of the ratio of their radii.

4. **Final Ratio**:
   - The ratio of the radii is \(\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\phi^2}} = \phi^2\).
   - Therefore, the ratio of the areas is \((\phi^2)^2 = \phi^4\).

Thus, the ratio of the area of the original pentagon to the smaller pentagon is \(\boxed{\phi^4}\).<#sep#> '..'?

tool: ./.eggs/pytest_runner-6.0.1-py3<#sep#>Find the longest geometric progression with a common ratio greater than 1 that can be formed from the set \( \{100, 101, 102, \ldots, 1000\} \). What is the length of this progression?

Okay, so I need to find the longest geometric progression (GP) with a common ratio greater than 1 from the set {100, 101, 102, ..., 1000}. The question is asking for the length of the longest such progression. Hmm, let's start by recalling what a geometric progression is. It's a sequence where each term after the first is found by multiplying the previous term by a constant called the common ratio (r). Since r is greater than 1, each term will be larger than the previous one.

First, I need to figure out how to approach this. Maybe I should start by considering possible common ratios and seeing how many terms I can get within the given set for each ratio. The longer the progression, the better. Let me think. The set starts at 100 and ends at 1000, so the possible terms are integers from 100 to 1000 inclusive. The common ratio has to be a number such that when we multiply the starting term by r multiple times, we stay within the set. Since r > 1, the terms increase, so we need to make sure that the last term doesn't exceed 1000.

Let me denote the first term as a, and the common ratio as r. Then the terms of the GP are: a, ar, ar^2, ar^3, ..., ar^{n-1}, where n is the number of terms. All these terms must be integers within [100, 1000]. So, for a GP to be valid, each term ar^k must be an integer in the given range. Therefore, both a and r must be such that all terms are integers. Wait, but the common ratio r can be a rational number, right? For example, if a is divisible by the denominator of r when expressed in simplest terms, then each subsequent term could still be an integer. However, dealing with rational ratios might complicate things. Maybe starting with integer ratios would be easier? Hmm, but the problem doesn't specify that the ratio has to be an integer, just greater than 1. So we need to consider both integer and non-integer ratios. But since all terms must be integers, the ratio must be a rational number. Because if r is irrational, then multiplying an integer a by an irrational number would result in an irrational term, which is not in the set. So r must be rational. Let's denote r as p/q where p and q are positive integers with p > q (since r > 1) and gcd(p, q) = 1.

So, to have all terms as integers, the initial term a must be divisible by q^{k} for the k-th term. Wait, let's think about that. If the ratio is p/q, then the second term is a*(p/q), which must be an integer. Therefore, a must be divisible by q. The third term is a*(p/q)^2, so a must be divisible by q^2. The fourth term is a*(p/q)^3, so a must be divisible by q^3, and so on. Therefore, for a GP of length n with ratio p/q (in simplest terms), the starting term a must be divisible by q^{n-1}. Since our set is {100, 101, ..., 1000}, the starting term a must be at least 100, and the last term a*(p/q)^{n-1} must be at most 1000.

So, the problem reduces to finding integers a, p, q, n such that:

1. a ≥ 100
2. a*(p/q)^{n-1} ≤ 1000
3. a is divisible by q^{n-1}
4. p > q ≥ 1, gcd(p, q) = 1
5. All intermediate terms a*(p/q)^k for k = 1, 2, ..., n-1 are integers in [100, 1000]

Our goal is to maximize n.

This seems a bit complex. Maybe trying to approach it by checking possible ratios. Let me first check for integer ratios because they might be easier to handle. For integer ratios r, the terms are a, a*r, a*r^2, ..., a*r^{n-1}, and all these must be in [100, 1000]. So, the maximum possible n for a given r is the largest integer such that a*r^{n-1} ≤ 1000. Since a must be at least 100, the minimal starting term is 100. So, starting from a=100, with ratio r, the maximum n would be floor(log_r(1000/100)) + 1 = floor(log_r(10)) + 1. For example, if r=2, log2(10) is approx 3.3219, so floor(3.3219) = 3, so n=4. Let's check: 100, 200, 400, 800, next would be 1600 which is over. So indeed 4 terms. Similarly, for r=3: log3(10) ≈ 2.095, floor(2.095) = 2, so n=3. 100, 300, 900, next would be 2700. So 3 terms.

But maybe starting with a higher a can allow more terms? For example, if a is smaller than 100, but multiplied by r to reach 100. But the problem states the progression must be formed from the set {100, 101, ..., 1000}, so all terms must be in that set. Therefore, the starting term a must be at least 100.      1	'''
     2	This file defines the parser class that is used to parse a given SCIM query.
     3	
     4	See https://tools.ietf.org/html/rfc7644#section-3.4.2.2 for more details
     5	
     6	::
     7	
     8	    SCIM filters MUST conform to the following ABNF [RFC5234] rules as
     9	    specified below:
    10	
    11	      FILTER    = attrExp / logExp / valuePath / *1"not" "(" FILTER ")"
    12	
    13	      valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
    14	                  ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
    15	
    16	      valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
    17	
    18	      attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
    19	                  (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
    20	
    21	      logExp    = FILTER SP ("and" / "or") SP FILTER
    22	
    23	      compValue = false / null / true / number / string
    24	                  ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
    25	
    26	      compareOp = "eq" / "ne" / "co" /
    27	                         "sw" / "ew" /
    28	                         "gt" / "lt" /
    29	                         "ge" / "le"
    30	
    31	      attrPath  = [URI ":"] ATTRNAME *1subAttr
    32	                  ; SCIM attribute name
    33	                  ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
    34	
    35	      ATTRNAME  = ALPHA *(nameChar)
    36	
    37	      nameChar  = "-" / "_" / DIGIT / ALPHA
    38	
    39	      subAttr   = "." ATTRNAME
    40	                  ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
    41	
    42	                Figure 1: ABNF Specification of SCIM Filters
    43	
    44	    In the above ABNF rules, the "compValue" (comparison value) rule is
    45	    built on JSON Data Interchange format ABNF rules as specified in
    46	    [RFC7159], "DIGIT" and "ALPHA" are defined per Appendix B.1 of
    47	    [RFC5234], and "URI" is defined per Appendix A of [RFC3986].

    50	    order of precedence:
    51	
    52	    1.  Grouping operators
    53

    55	        which takes precedence over "or"
    56	
    57	    3.  Attribute operators
    58	
    59	    If the specified attribute in a filter expression is a multi-valued
    60	    attribute, the filter matches if any of the values of the specified
    61	    attribute match the specified criterion; e.g., if a User has multiple
    62	    "emails" values, only one has to match for the entire User to match.
    63	    For complex attributes, a fully qualified sub-attribute MUST be
    64	    specified using standard attribute notation (Section 3.10).  For
    65	    example, to filter by userName, the parameter value is "userName".
    66	    To filter by first name, the parameter value is "name.givenName".
    67	
    68	    When applying a comparison (e.g., "eq") or presence filter (e.g.,
    69	    "pr") to a defaulted attribute, the service provider SHALL use the
    70	    value that was returned to the client that last created or modified
    71	    the attribute.
    72

    74	    Providers MUST decline to filter results if the specified filter
    75	    operation is not recognized and return an HTTP 400 error with a
    76	    "scimType" error of "invalidFilter" and an appropriate human-readable
    77	    response as per Section 3.12.  For example, if a client specified an

Hmm this is getting too convoluted. I think the key is that the reference approach uses (c +5) spaces for the push, which makes each character's contribution as (c +5 +1) + (2) → total c+8 bytes. 

Now, to improve on this, the problem is to find shorter ways than this for each character or sequences of characters.

The task is to find a way to have the Starry program compute and push numbers more efficiently without needing to have, say, 72 spaces for code point 72.

Possible strategies:

1. Compute numbers using arithmetic operations (+ - * div mod), so that we can combine numbers via addition etc. and thereby have shorter instruction that build the numbers.

2. Using loops with labels to repeat operations for certain counts, which saves on the number of bytes needed for repeated operations.

3. If the string has repeated characters or patterns, you can compute their codes once and repeat printing them with jumps etc. But considering that some test cases are random, this may not always apply, but adding this may help in some cases.

4. Use 0 as a base, repeatedly adding a delta made by the processor, so that increments/decrements can build up numbers.

But first, let's think of the most straightforward way to represent individual numbers.

Starry's stack-based approach means that to push a value, one can use:

- '+' instruction (Push)

but >5 spaces.

But alternatives:

Suppose we have 0 on the stack, then push a +5 with + (but it's pushing a number only when space counts )

Alternatively, combining existing stack elements with arithmetic.

The core idea is to find efficient ways to compute each code point by combining previous numbers on the stack with operations.

Initially, the stack is empty.

To push a value, you can either push numbers via '+' command with spaces (n) where n >=5. 

Alternatively, once you have some numbers on the stack, you can perform operations like addition (via the * instruction with proper spacing).

For instance:

Example to push 3:

'    +' (4 spaces? 4 's? Wait, no '+ with  spaces n where n ≧5 will push (n-5). 

Wait, the + instruction pushed n-5 when there are n spaces, but n must be at least 5.

So for push 3, need 8 spaces → '        ' + (8 spaces is 8-5=3.

Alternatively, can we compute 3 from something else?

Like, using arithmetic operations, perhaps starting from 0.

But how do you get zero? 

Wait, there is an instruction 4+ → which is:

When spaces count is 4 in the '+', then you pop and discard the top stack element.

Hmm not helpful for getting zeros. 

Alternatively, you can set up a stack with a known value and then operate.

For example, suppose pushing a 5, then using arithmetic to build other numbers.

Example, to build 10:

Push 5, duplicated and added. 

But to do that:

1. push 5 → with 10 spaces (5+5 =10) → '10 spaces ' '+ 

2. then for adding them, use 0 mod5 * (add MN):

The * command with 0 mod5 spaces → mod5 spaces must be congruent 0 → spaces counts are 0 mod5.

Thus, after pushing 5 twice (stack [5,5]), then using the add command (spaces mod5=0) → which adds (5 +5 =10)

Thus:

The steps to make 10 might be:

Push 5: _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _+ (10 spaces) → that's 11 bytes.

    99	class SCIMParser(Parser):
   100	    # debugfile = 'debug.log'
   101	
   102	    # Same token set as defined in the lexer
   103	    tokens = lexer.SCIMLexer.tokens
   104	
   105	    # Operator precedence table.
   106	    #   Filters MUST be evaluated using the following order of operations, in
   107	    #   order of precedence:
   108	    #   1.  Grouping operators
   109	    #   2.  Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and",
   110	    #       which takes precedence over "or"
   111	    #   3.  Attribute operators
   112	    precedence = (
   113	        ('nonassoc', OR),  # noqa F821
   114	        ('nonassoc', AND), # noqa F821
   115	        ('nonassoc', NOT), # noqa F821
   116	    )
   117	
   118	    # FILTER    = attrExp / logExp / valuePath / *1"not" "(" FILTER ")"
   119	    #                                           ; 0 or 1 "not"s
   120	    @_('attr_exp')  # noqa F821
   121	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   122	        return ast.Filter(p.attr_exp, False, None)
   123	
   124	    @_('log_exp')  # noqa F821
   125	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   126	        return ast.Filter(p.log_exp, False, None)

   130	        return ast.Filter(p.value_path, False, None)

   132	    @_('LPAREN filter RPAREN', # noqa F821
   133	       'NOT LPAREN filter RPAREN')
   134	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   135	        negate = p[0].lower() == 'not'
   136	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, negate, None)
   137	
   138	    # valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
   139	    #            ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
   140	    # valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
   141	    #                               ; 0 or 1 "not"s
   142	    #
   143	    # Since valFilter and Filter have the same structure, we have to
   144	    # parse valFilter as a Filter. Else we'll run into reduce/reduce conflicts.
   145	    #
   146	    # attr_path here acts like a namespace for the filter operation inside
   147	    # the brackets. Thus we need to distribute the namespace to the leaf nodes
   148	    # of the filter node. We will attach attr_path to the filter and use
   149	    # it in the transpiler.
   150	    @_('attr_path LBRACKET filter RBRACKET') # noqa F821
   151	    def value_path(self, p):
   152	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, False, p.attr_path)

   168	        return ast.AttrExpr(p[1], p.attr_path, comp_value)
   169

   171	    @_('filter OR filter', # noqa F821

assistant: Perfect! The RFC clearly states the precedence order:
1. Grouping operators (parentheses)
2. Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and", which takes precedence over "or"
3. Attribute operators

So the precedence should be (from highest to lowest):
1. NOT
2. AND  
3. OR

And both AND and OR should be left-associative to allow chaining.

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS

### 5.1 Problem Statement
The SCIM filter parser fails when parsing queries with multiple consecutive "or" or "and" operators because these operators are defined as non-associative in the precedence table.

### 5.2 Problem Location
The issue is in `/testbed/src/scim2_filter_parser/parser.py` at lines 112-116 where the precedence is defined:

```python
precedence = (
    ('nonassoc', OR),  # noqa F821
    ('nonassoc', AND), # noqa F821
    ('nonassoc', NOT), # noqa F821
)
```

### 5.3 How the Test Reproduces the Issue
The reproduction script shows that queries with 3+ consecutive operators of the same type fail with parsing errors, while mixed operator queries work fine.

### 5.4 Best Practices for the Fix
1. Follow the RFC 7644 specification for operator precedence
2. Make logical operators left-associative to allow chaining
3. Maintain backward compatibility with existing working queries
4. Ensure proper precedence order: NOT > AND > OR

### 5.5 How to Fix the Problem
Change the precedence definition to make AND and OR left-associative and properly ordered:

```python
precedence = (
    ('left', OR),   # Lowest precedence
    ('left', AND),  # Higher than OR
    ('right', NOT), # Highest precedence
)
```

This will allow expressions like `A or B or C` to be parsed as `(A or B) or C` and ensure proper precedence.

To find the longest geometric progression (GP) with a common ratio greater than 1 from the set \( \{100, 101, 102, \ldots, 1000\} \), we need to consider sequences where each term is an integer within the given range. The key steps are as follows:

1. **Identify the common ratio**: The common ratio \( r \) must be a rational number (since all terms must be integers) and greater than 1. We consider ratios in the form \( \frac{p}{q} \) where \( p \) and \( q \) are coprime integers.

2. **Check integer ratios**: For integer ratios, the maximum number of terms is calculated by starting from the smallest term (100) and multiplying by the ratio until exceeding 1000. However, integer ratios yield fewer terms compared to some rational ratios.

3. **Consider rational ratios**: For a ratio \( \frac{3}{2} \), the starting term must be a multiple of \( 2^{n-1} \) to ensure all terms are integers. The smallest such term within the range is 128 (which is \( 2^7 \)). 

4. **Verify the sequence**: Starting at 128 with a ratio of \( \frac{3}{2} \), the terms are:
   \[
   128, 192, 288, 432, 648, 972
   \]
   The next term would be \( 972 \times \frac{3}{2} = 1458 \), which exceeds 1000. This gives a sequence of 6 terms.

5. **Check other possible ratios**: Other rational ratios such as \( \frac{4}{3} \), \( \frac{5}{4} \), and others were considered, but they resulted in shorter sequences or non-integer terms.

6. **Conclusion**: The longest geometric progression found has 6 terms with a common ratio of \( \frac{3}{2} \) starting from 128.

Thus, the length of the longest geometric progression is \(\boxed{6}\).<#sep#>- Direct 的 cost: (val +5) +1 →因为 the spaces are val +5→

## Phase 1. READING: Understanding the Problem



tool: Your thought has been logged.

assistant: ### 1.1-1.3 Problem Analysis

The issue is with the SCIM (System for Cross-domain Identity Management) filter parser. The parser fails when processing queries with three or more consecutive "or" operators or three or more consecutive "and" operators.

**Key technical details:**
- **Error**: `scim2_filter_parser.parser.SCIMParserError`
- **Error location**: Token at position index=52 
- **Token type**: OR (but likely affects AND as well)
- **Module**: `scim2_filter_parser.parser`

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce

1. Create a query with 3+ consecutive "or" operators:
   `"displayName co \"username\" or nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""`
2. Create a query with 3+ consecutive "and" operators:
   `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" and userName co \"username\""`
3. Both should fail with parsing error
4. A mixed query like `"displayName co \"username\" and nickName co \"username\" or userName co \"username\""` works

### 1.5 Best Practices for Testing and Fixing

- Understand the grammar rules for operator precedence and associativity
- Ensure left-to-right evaluation for operators of same precedence
- Test edge cases with multiple operators
- Maintain backward compatibility with existing working queries

    51	    Token(type='ATTRNAME', value='type', lineno=<#sep#>Find the expectation \( \mathbb{E}[X_t] \) where \( X_t = \sin(B_t) \) and \( B_t \) is a standard Brownian motion for \( t \geq 0 \).

Okay, so I need to find the expectation of X_t, which is defined as sin(B_t), where B_t is a standard Brownian motion. Hmm, let me start by recalling what a standard Brownian motion is. It's a continuous-time stochastic process starting at 0, with independent increments, and each increment is normally distributed with mean 0 and variance equal to the time difference. So, B_t is a normal random variable with mean 0 and variance t. That means B_t ~ N(0, t). 

Therefore, the problem reduces to finding the expected value of sin(Z) where Z is a normal random variable with mean 0 and variance t. So, essentially, E[sin(Z)] where Z ~ N(0, t). I need to compute this expectation. 

Let me recall that for a normal random variable Z with mean μ and variance σ², the expectation E[e^{iZ}] is e^{iμ - σ²/2}. This is the characteristic function of a normal distribution. But here, I have sin(Z) instead of e^{iZ}. However, maybe I can express sin(Z) in terms of exponentials using Euler's formula: sin(z) = (e^{iz} - e^{-iz})/(2i). So, perhaps I can use that to compute the expectation.

Let's try that. Let me write:

E[sin(Z)] = E[(e^{iZ} - e^{-iZ})/(2i)] = [E[e^{iZ}] - E[e^{-iZ}]]/(2i).

Since Z ~ N(0, t), then the characteristic function is E[e^{iλZ}] = e^{-λ² t /2}. So, for λ = 1, E[e^{iZ}] = e^{-(1)^2 t /2} = e^{-t/2}. Similarly, for λ = -1, E[e^{-iZ}] = e^{-(-1)^2 t /2} = e^{-t/2}. Therefore, both expectations are equal to e^{-t/2}.

Therefore, substituting back into the expression for E[sin(Z)]:

[ e^{-t/2} - e^{-t/2} ]/(2i) = 0/(2i) = 0.

Wait, so does that mean the expectation is 0? That seems too straightforward. Let me check again.

Yes, because sin is an odd function, and the distribution of Z is symmetric around 0 (since it's a mean 0 normal distribution). Therefore, the integral of sin(z) times the symmetric PDF should be zero. That makes sense. So, the expectation E[sin(B_t)] is 0.

But let me verify this another way to be sure. Let's compute the integral directly. The expectation is the integral from -∞ to ∞ of sin(z) * (1/√(2πt)) e^{-z²/(2t)} dz. Since sin(z) is an odd function and the exponential function is even, their product is an odd function. The integral of an odd function over a symmetric interval around zero is zero. Therefore, the expectation is indeed zero. 

Therefore, regardless of t, the expectation E[X_t] = E[sin(B_t)] = 0. 

Wait, but let me consider if t=0. If t=0, then B_0 = 0, so sin(B_0) = sin(0) = 0, so expectation is 0. For t > 0, since B_t is N(0,t), same reasoning applies. So, yes, the expectation is zero for all t ≥ 0. 

Therefore, the answer should be 0. But let me just think if there's any mistake in this reasoning. For example, could there be some issue with the integrability or convergence? But sin(z) is bounded between -1 and 1, and the normal distribution has all moments, so the integral should definitely converge, and since it's an odd function integrated over symmetric interval, it's zero. 

Alternatively, maybe using Ito's lemma? Although that might complicate things more than necessary. Let me try that approach for verification. 

If I consider X_t = sin(B_t). Then, by Ito's formula, dX_t = cos(B_t) dB_t - (1/2) sin(B_t) dt. Therefore, the differential of X_t has a drift term -(1/2) sin(B_t) dt and a diffusion term cos(B_t) dB_t. 

Therefore, integrating from 0 to t, we have:

X_t = X_0 + ∫₀ᵗ cos(B_s) dB_s - (1/2) ∫₀ᵗ sin(B_s) ds.

Taking expectation on both sides:

E[X_t] = E[X_0] + E[∫₀ᵗ cos(B_s) dB_s] - (1/2) E[∫₀ᵗ sin(B_s) ds].

Now, X_0 = sin(B_0) = sin(0) = 0, so E[X_0] = 0. The expectation of the stochastic integral ∫₀ᵗ cos(B_s) dB_s is zero because it's a martingale (the integrand is adapted and square-integrable). Then, the last term is -(1/2) ∫₀ᵗ E[sin(B_s)] ds. 

But this leads to E[X_t] = - (1/2) ∫₀ᵗ E[sin(B_s)] ds. Let me denote m(t) = E[X_t] = E[sin(B_t)]. Then, the equation becomes:

m(t) = - (1/2) ∫₀ᵗ m(s) ds.

This is an integral equation for m(t). Differentiating both sides with respect to t, we get:

m'(t) = - (1/2) m(t).

This is a first-order linear ordinary differential equation. The solution is m(t) = m(0) e^{- (1/2) t}. But m(0) = E[sin(B_0)] = sin(0) = 0. Therefore, m(t) = 0 for all t. 

But wait, this contradicts with the previous reasoning? Wait, no. Wait, using both methods, we get m(t) = 0. Wait, no. But m(0) = 0, so m(t) = 0 for all t. So, that also gives E[X_t] = 0. So, both methods confirm that the expectation is zero. 

Therefore, that seems to solidify the conclusion. 

But wait, let me check with a concrete example. Suppose t is very small. Let’s take t approaching 0. Then B_t is approximately N(0, t), which is a small normal variable. Then sin(B_t) ≈ B_t - (B_t)^3/6 + ... So, E[sin(B_t)] ≈ E[B_t] - E[B_t^3]/6 + ... Since E[B_t] = 0, and E[B_t^3] = 0 (because odd moments of normal distribution are zero). Similarly, all the odd terms in the expansion will have zero expectation. Therefore, the expectation is zero. 

Alternatively, if t is large, even though B_t can take large values, sin(B_t) is still bounded between -1 and 1, and due to the symmetry of the normal distribution around zero, positive and negative areas cancel out. So, overall, the expectation remains zero. 

Therefore, all lines of reasoning point towards the expectation being zero. Therefore, I can be confident that the answer is zero.

To find the expectation \( \mathbb{E}[X_t] \) where \( X_t = \sin(B_t) \) and \( B_t \) is a standard Brownian motion, we start by noting that \( B_t \) is a normal random variable with mean 0 and variance \( t \). Therefore, we need to compute \( \mathbb{E}[\sin(Z)] \) where \( Z \sim N(0, t) \).

\[

Her house is a N-dimensional  hypercube, with the centre located in the origin, with each edge having length equal to 2. There is a room in every vertex of the hypercube. The room can be denoted with N it's coordinates. For any two rooms, there is a corridor between them if the square of the euclidean distance is no more than D units. 
Sometimes, Jane wants to make a clean-up in her house. In order to do that, she needs to visit all the rooms. She starts with a room with the coordinates (S1, S2, ... SN) and then wants to move through all the rooms via corridors in such a way that she will visit all the rooms, and, at the same time, won't visit any room twice (she does not want to step on a floor which is not dried yet).
Please find such a route for Jane or state that it's impossible to find one.
Input
The first line of the input contains an integer T denoting the number of test cases. The description of T test cases follows.
The first line of each test case contains two space separated integers N and D denoting the number of dimensions of the space Jane lives in and the square of the maximal euclidean distance between two rooms, connected via corridor.
The second line contains N space-separated integers S1, S2, ..., SN denoting the coordinates of the room where Jane starts the cleaning.

Output
For each test case output:

if the a route exists output 2^N lines, each containing N space separated integers, denoting the coordinates of the corresponding room in the route. For every test case, the coordinates of the first room in the route should coincide with the given location. If there is more than one solution, you can print any one of them.
if such a route doesn't exist, output just -2 on the separate line.


Constraints
Example
Input:
2
2 5
-1 -1
4 3
1 -1 1 -1

Output:
-1 -1
1 -1
1 1
-1 1
-2


Explanation

Example case 2. It is clearly impossible to accomplish the task.

Therefore, we need to compute \( \mathbb{E}[\sin(Z)] \) where \( Z \sim N(0, t) \).

Using Euler's formula, we express \( \sin(Z) \) as:
\[
\sin(Z) = \frac{e^{iZ} - e^{-iZ}}{2i}
\]
Taking the expectation, we get:
\[
\mathbb{E}[\sin(Z)] = \mathbb{E}\left[\frac{e^{iZ} - e^{-iZ}}{2i}\right] = \frac{1}{2i} \left( \mathbb{E}[e^{iZ}] - \mathbb{E}[e^{-iZ}] \right)
\]
The characteristic function of a normal random variable \( Z \sim N(0, t) \) is \( \mathbb{E}[e^{i\lambda Z}] = e^{-\lambda^2 t / 2} \). Substituting \( \lambda = 1 \) and \( \lambda = -1 \), we get:
\[
\mathbb{E}[e^{iZ}] = e^{-t/2} \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbb{E}[e^{-iZ}] = e^{-t/2}
\]
Thus,
\[
\mathbb{E}[\sin(Z)] = \frac{1}{2i} \left( e^{-t/2} - e^{-t/2} \right) = 0
\]

We can also verify this using the symmetry of the normal distribution. The function \( \sin(z) \) is odd, and the probability density function of \( Z \) is even. The product of an odd function and an even function is odd, and the integral of an odd function over a symmetric interval around zero is zero. Hence, the expectation is zero.

Additionally, using Itô's lemma on \( X_t = \sin(B_t) \), we derive the stochastic differential equation:
\[
dX_t = \cos(B_t) dB_t - \frac{1}{2} \sin(B_t) dt
\]
Taking expectations, we find:
\[
\mathbb{E}[X_t] = \mathbb{E}[X_0] + \mathbb{E}\left[\int_0^t \cos(B_s) dB_s\right] - \frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \mathbb{E}[\sin(B_s)] ds
\]
Since the expectation of the stochastic integral is zero and \( \mathbb{E}[X_0] = 0 \), we get:
\[
\mathbb{E}[X_t] = -\frac{1}{2} \int_0^t \mathbb{E}[\sin(B_s)] ds
\]
Letting \( m(t) = \mathbb{E}[X_t] \), we solve the integral equation:
\[
m(t) = -\frac{1}{2} \int_0^t m(s) ds
\]
Differentiating both sides, we obtain the ODE \( m'(t) = -\frac{1}{2} m(t) \), with solution \( m(t) = m(0) e^{-t/2} \). Since \( m(0) = 0 \), we conclude \( m(t) = 0 \) for all \( t \).

Thus, the expectation is:
\[

   190	
   191	If you would like to produce a `Django Q`_ object instead of a raw SQL query, you can pass
   192	a SCIM filter query and attribute map to the ``get_query`` function from the module
   193	``scim2_filter_parser.transpilers.django_q_object``. For example::
   194	
   195	    get_query(scim_query: str, attr_map: Mapping)
   196	
   197	This Q object can then be passed to a Django filter query like so::
   198	
   199	    query = get_query(scim_query, attr_map)
   200	    User.objects.filter(query)
   201	
   202	Please note that you will need to install the Django Query extra like for this feature to be available::
   203	
   204	    pip install scim2-filter-parser[django-query]
   205	
   206	.. _`Django Q`: https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/3.1/topics/db/queries/#complex-lookups-with-q-objects
   207	
   208	Speed
   209	-----
   210	
   211	SFP is pretty fast. Check out the speed_test.py script for details on the long and short
   212	filter queries tested. SFP transpiled a short filter query into SQL in under 54 microseconds.
   213	For a longer query, SFP only took 273 microseconds.
   214	
   215	::
   216	
   217	    ➜  scim2-filter-parser git:(master) ✗ python -m timeit -s "import speed_test" "speed_test.short()"
   218	    10000 loops, best of 3: 53.8 usec per loop
   219	    ➜  scim2-filter-parser git:(master) ✗ python -m timeit -s "import speed_test" "speed_test.long()"
   220	    1000 loops, best of 3: 273 usec per loop
   221	
   222	Development Speed
   223	-----------------

   225	Since this project is relatively stable, time is only dedicated to it on
   226	Fridays. Thus if you issue a PR, bug, etc, please note that it may take a week
   227	before we get back to you. Thanks you for your patience.

How can we generate such a path?

Well, hypercubes have many known Hamiltonian paths and cycles, and one method to traverse them. One common way is using a Gray code sequence which ensures each subsequent entry differs by one bit (so edge of the hypercube). A Gray code for N bits lists all 2^N binary numbers, each differing in exactly one bit from the prior. Since in this problem, we can represent the coordinates as ±1, so each coordinate bit can be considered as a binary 0/1, where 0 corresponds to -1 and 1 to +1.

So the idea is to:

1. Encode the coordinates as binary 0/1, converting S coordinates to their bit pattern (e.g., -1 is 0, +1 is 1).

2. Find a Gray code sequence starting with the given bit pattern (corresponding to S).

3. Convert each Gray code entry back to the ±1 coordinates.

Then, the Gray code path is a Hamiltonian path (as each adjacent entry differs by one coordinate, thus in the hypercube's edges), and hence uses edges of squared distance 4 (since m=1) which satisfies the D >=4 condition.

However, since D can be larger than 4, but allowing higher m steps, but the path needs to use only edges allowed, but even a Gray code path (which uses only edges of difference exactly m=1) will be acceptable since D is >=4. Even if D is higher than 4, you can still traverse via the Gray code path.

Since the discriminant is negative, the quadratic equation has no real roots, indicating that \( f(x) \) has no critical points. This means \( f(x) \) is strictly monotonic (either always increasing or always decreasing). Given the leading coefficient of \( f(x) \) is positive, the function is strictly increasing.

A strictly increasing cubic function crosses the x-axis exactly once, implying the equation \( x^3 - 3x^2 + 6x + k = 0 \) has exactly one real root for any value of \( k \).

    16	      valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
    17	
    18	      attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
    19	                  (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
    20	
    21	      logExp    = FILTER SP ("and" / "or") SP FILTER
    22	
    23	      compValue = false / null / true / number / string
    24	                  ; rules from JSON (RFC 7159)
    25	
    26	      compareOp = "eq" / "ne" / "co" /
    27	                         "sw" / "ew" /
    28	                         "gt" / "lt" /
    29	                         "ge" / "le"
    30	
    31	      attrPath  = [URI ":"] ATTRNAME *1subAttr
    32	                  ; SCIM attribute name
    33	                  ; URI is SCIM "schema" URI
    34	
    35	      ATTRNAME  = ALPHA *(nameChar)
    36	
    37	      nameChar  = "-" / "_" / DIGIT / ALPHA
    38	
    39	      subAttr   = "." ATTRNAME
    40	                  ; a sub-attribute of a complex attribute
    41	
    42	                Figure 1: ABNF Specification of SCIM Filters
    43	
    44	    In the above ABNF rules, the "compValue" (comparison value) rule is
    45	    built on JSON Data Interchange format ABNF rules as specified in
    46	    [RFC7159], "DIGIT" and "ALPHA" are defined per Appendix B.1 of
    47	    [RFC5234], and "URI" is defined per Appendix A of [RFC3986].
    48	
    49	    Filters MUST be evaluated using the following order of operations, in
    50	    order of precedence:
    51	
    52	    1.  Grouping operators
    53	
    54	    2.  Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and",
    55	        which takes precedence over "or"
    56	
    57	    3.  Attribute operators
    58	
    59	    If the specified attribute in a filter expression is a multi-valued
    60	    attribute, the filter matches if any of the values of the specified
    61	    attribute match the specified criterion; e.g., if a User has multiple
    62	    "emails" values, only one has to match for the entire User to match.
    63	    For complex attributes, a fully qualified sub-attribute MUST be
    64	    specified using standard attribute notation (Section 3.10).  For
    65	    example, to filter by userName, the parameter value is "userName".
    66	    To filter by first name, the parameter value is "name.givenName".
    67	
    68	    When applying a comparison (e.g., "eq") or presence filter (e.g.,
    69	    "pr") to a defaulted attribute, the service provider SHALL use the
    70	    value that was returned to the client that last created or modified
    71	    the attribute.
    72	
    73	    Providers MAY support additional filter operations if they choose.
    74	    Providers MUST decline to filter results if the specified filter
    75	    operation is not recognized and return an HTTP 400 error with a
    76	    "scimType" error of "invalidFilter" and an appropriate human-readable
    77	    response as per Section 3.12.  For example, if a client specified an
    78	    unsupported operator named 'regex', the service provider should
    79	    specify an error response description identifying the client error,
    80	    e.g., 'The operator 'regex' is not supported.'
    81	
    82	    When comparing attributes of type String, the case sensitivity for
    83	    String type attributes SHALL be determined by the attribute's
    84	    "caseExact" characteristic (see Section 2.2 of [RFC7643]).
    85	
    86	    Clients MAY query by schema or schema extensions by using a filter
    87	    expression including the "schemas" attribute (as shown in Figure 2).
    88	'''
    89	from sly import Parser
    90	
    91	from . import ast
    92	from . import lexer
    93	
    94	
    95	class SCIMParserError(Exception):
    96	    pass
    97	
    98	
    99	class SCIMParser(Parser):
   100	    # debugfile = 'debug.log'
   101	
   102	    # Same token set as defined in the lexer
   103	    tokens = lexer.SCIMLexer.tokens
   104	
   105	    # Operator precedence table.
   106	    #   Filters MUST be evaluated using the following order of operations, in
   107	    #   order of precedence:
   108	    #   1.  Grouping operators
   109	    #   2.  Logical operators - where "not" takes precedence over "and",
   110	    #       which takes precedence over "or"
   111	    #   3.  Attribute operators
   112	    precedence = (
   113	        ('nonassoc', OR),  # noqa F821
   114	        ('nonassoc', AND), # noqa F821
   115	        ('nonassoc', NOT), # noqa F821
   116	    )
   117	
   118	    # FILTER    = attrExp / logExp / valuePath / *1"not" "(" FILTER ")"
   119	    #                                           ; 0 or 1 "not"s
   120	    @_('attr_exp')  # noqa F821
   121	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   122	        return ast.Filter(p.attr_exp, False, None)
   123	
   124	    @_('log_exp')  # noqa F821
   125	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   126	        return ast.Filter(p.log_exp, False, None)
   127	
   128	    @_('value_path')  # noqa F821
   129	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   130	        return ast.Filter(p.value_path, False, None)
   131	
   132	    @_('LPAREN filter RPAREN', # noqa F821
   133	       'NOT LPAREN filter RPAREN')
   134	    def filter(self, p):  # noqa F811
   135	        negate = p[0].lower() == 'not'
   136	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, negate, None)
   137	
   138	    # valuePath = attrPath "[" valFilter "]"
   139	    #            ; FILTER uses sub-attributes of a parent attrPath
   140	    # valFilter = attrExp / logExp / *1"not" "(" valFilter ")"
   141	    #                               ; 0 or 1 "not"s
   142	    #
   143	    # Since valFilter and Filter have the same structure, we have to
   144	    # parse valFilter as a Filter. Else we'll run into reduce/reduce conflicts.
   145	    #
   146	    # attr_path here acts like a namespace for the filter operation inside
   147	    # the brackets. Thus we need to distribute the namespace to the leaf nodes
   148	    # of the filter node. We will attach attr_path to the filter and use
   149	    # it in the transpiler.
   150	    @_('attr_path LBRACKET filter RBRACKET') # noqa F821
   151	    def value_path(self, p):
   152	        return ast.Filter(p.filter, False, p.attr_path)
   153	
   154	    # attrExp   = (attrPath SP "pr") /
   155	    #             (attrPath SP compareOp SP compValue)
   156	    @_('attr_path PR', # noqa F821
   157	       'attr_path EQ comp_value',
   158	       'attr_path NE comp_value',
   159	       'attr_path CO comp_value',
   160	       'attr_path SW comp_value',
   161	       'attr_path EW comp_value',
   162	       'attr_path GT comp_value',
